L46: ## 1 EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
L47: ## 1.1. Product Introduction
L48: Nirogacestat (OGSIVEO®) is an oral, small molecule \gamma-secretase inhibitor. At the time of the New Drug Application (NDA) submission on December 23, 2022, nirogacestat was not approved for any indication in the United States or elsewhere. The Applicant's proposed indication at the time of the NDA submission was:
L49: OGSIVEO is a gamma secretase inhibitor indicated for the treatment of adults with desmoid tumors.
L50: The proposed dosing regimen is 150 mg (3 × 50 mg tablets) administered orally twice daily (BID).
L51: ## 1.2. Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L52: The recommendation for **traditional approval** of nirogacestat in patients with desmoid tumors (DT) is based on the results of the NIR-DT-301 study, an international, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study in 142 adult patients with progressing DTs which demonstrated a statistically significant and clinically meaningful improvement in progression-free survival (PFS) and objective response rate (ORR) in patients treated with nirogacestat over placebo including supportive patient-reported outcome data for pain.
L53: Substantial evidence of effectiveness was established with one adequate and well controlled investigation (i.e., Study NIR-DT-301), with highly persuasive results, that is considered to be the scientific equivalent of two clinical investigations.
L54: Patients (n = 142) in Study NIR-DT-301 were randomized 1:1 to receive nirogacestat or placebo until disease progression, unacceptable toxicity, or withdrawal from the study for other reasons. Cross-over from placebo to nirogacestat was allowed at the time of radiographic disease progression (patients remained blinded without allowing for cross over for clinical progression events). Cross-over was also permitted following the primary PFS analysis.
L55: The primary efficacy endpoint was PFS determined by
L56: - Blinded Independent Central Imaging Review (BICR) using Response Evaluation Criteria in Solid Tumors (RECIST) v1.1, or
- The onset or worsening of symptoms resulting in a global deterioration of health status causing the permanent discontinuation from study treatment and the initiation of emergent treatment (e.g., radiotherapy, surgery, or systemic therapy including chemotherapy or tyrosine kinase inhibitors) for DT/AF.
L57: The key secondary endpoints were objective response rate by central review; and the following patient-reported outcomes (PROs) measures:
L58: - Brief Pain Inventory (BPI) short form,
- GUnder/Desmoid Tumor Research Tumor Foundation (DTRF) DEsmoid Symptom/Impact Scale (GODDESS),
- Patient-Reported Outcomes Measurement Information System Physical Function (PROMIS PF) short form 10a plus 3 additional items from PROMIS item banks, and
- European Organisation for Research and Treatment of Cancer Quality of Life Questionnaire-Core 30 (EORTC) QLQ-C30 (EORTC QLQ-C30).
L59: Additional secondary endpoints or objectives included duration of response, comparing change in tumor volume measured by magnetic resonance imaging (MRI), and safety.
L60: During the development of nirogacestat, FDA expressed concerns regarding the inclusion of clinical progression as a PFS-defining event (e.g., the potential for bias exists in classifying a patient). Although bias may have been mitigated via the double-blind design, toxicities related to nirogacestat (e.g., GI toxicity, dermatological toxicities, ovarian suppression, or hypophosphatemia) may have allowed patients or clinicians to determine treatment assignment.
L61: The data submitted by the Applicant of an adequate and well-controlled trial provided substantial evidence for the effectiveness of nirogacestat administered at 150 mg BID versus placebo for the treatment of adult patients with progressing DTs who require systemic treatment and supports traditional the approval for the proposed indication. This conclusion is based on results from the NIR-DT-301 study which demonstrated a clinically and statistically significant improvement in PFS in patients who received nirogacestat compared to placebo with a hazard ratio (HR) of 0.29 (95% confidence interval [CI]: 0.15, 0.55), p &lt; 0.001.
L62: Concerns with respect to the inclusion of clinical progression events were mitigated via the following factors:
L63: - Nirogacestat demonstrated improved PFS in a sensitivity analysis based on radiographic assessment alone compared to placebo with a hazard ratio of 0.31 (95% CI: 0.16, 0.62). Notably most events observed in the trial were radiographic progression and not clinical progression.
L64: Category of PFS event	Nirogacestat
N=70	Placebo
N=72
Radiographic progression	11 (16%)	30 (42%)
Qualified clinical progression	1 (1%)	6 (8%)
Death before progression	0	1 (1%)
L65: - The assessment of tumor shrinkage (i.e., response rate per RECIST) favored the nirogacestat arm with an ORR of 41% (29.8, 53.8) for patients treated with nirogacestat versus 8% (3.1, 17.3) in patients who received placebo (note, the 95% CIs are based on FDA's analysis which differ slightly from the Applicant's).
- A key component of the quality of life for patients with DT is improvement in pain. Results from the BPI short form data were used as supportive evidence of the results of PFS. FDA conducted analyses on patients with clinical responses at various thresholds, and results were consistent in showing that more patients in the nirogacestat arm experienced pain improvement. Although other PRO results were also generally supportive of the nirogacestat treatment effect, missing PRO data precluded FDA from determining that specific PRO treatment effect sizes were accurate.
L66: In summary, although FDA had concerns regarding the inclusion of clinical progression events, overall, the totality of the data were highly persuasive with respect to benefit following nirogacestat treatment (in patients with DT who need systemic therapy).
L67: DT is a rare, serious condition that has significant morbidity and mortality, and has a high unmet medical need. A single randomized trial to support the approval of nirogacestat is acceptable in the context of the unmet medical need in the indicated population and the robustness of the efficacy results. Therefore, the review team recommends granting traditional approval to nirogacestat for the treatment of adult patients with progressing desmoid tumors who require systemic treatment.
L68: ## 1.3. Benefit-Risk Assessment (BRA)
L69: ## Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
L70: Desmoid tumors (DTs) are a rare subtype of soft tissue sarcoma (STS) that are locally aggressive and characterized by infiltrative growth which can affect surrounding vital organs causing significant impairment in the daily life of a patient due to chronic pain, functional deficits, psychological problems and decrease in quality of life (Kasper 2020). DTs have also been known to spontaneously regress. DTs have been reported to account for approximately 0.03% of all tumors (Reitamo 1982). They occur between the age of 15 and 60 years and with a peak age of about 30 years (Kasper 2011) and are twice as common in females as in males (Mishra). DTs are characterized by variable clinical behavior. Although most grow progressively larger over time, growth is usually indolent, and periods of growth arrest/spontaneous regression are not uncommon (Bonvalot 2013). The largest patient registry for DTs is the Desmoid Tumor Research Foundation Patient Registry and Natural History Study. In the first 15 months the registry was launched the ethnicity of 329 patients in the registry with DT was 92% White, 1.6% Black or African American, 1.6% Asian, 0.3% American Indian or Alaskan native, and 5% other. There have been two published DT studies that included the race and/or ethnicity. A COG clinical trial of sulindac and tamoxifen recruited 80% non-Hispanic (NH) or Latino, 14% Hispanic/Latino, 4% unknown ethnicity, 73% White, 16% Black or African American, 2.9% Asian, 1.4% Native Hawaiian or other Pacific Islander, and 7.1% unknown race (Mercier 2019). On the molecular level, DT's are characterized by mutations in the \beta-catenin gene, CTNNB1, or the adenomatous polyposis coli gene, APC (Kasper 2011). Most DTs arise sporadically, although between 5-15% are associated with familial adenomatous polyposis (FAP) caused by mutations in the APC gene. According to a retrospective review published in the American Society of Clinical Oncology, 96% of patients with DTs are alive 5 years after diagnosis, 92% after 10 years, and 87% after 15 years. The survival rates for patients with desmoid tumor vary based on several factors. These include the size of tumor, a patient's age and general health, and how well the treatment plan works. Other factors that can affect outcomes include where the tumor is located and how quickly the tumor grows. Intra-abdominal desmoid tumors are the most likely to cause potentially life-threatening complications, such as intestinal obstruction. DTs in the head and neck region can also be life-threatening (Ballo 2016).
L71: Diagnosis can be challenging as the clinical course can be unpredictable and typically requires a multidisciplinary team with expertise in treating DTs. There is no treatment approved by the FDA for DT; however, treatment guidelines reference available options, such as active surveillance, surgery, systemic therapy, and locoregional therapy. Choice of active treatment may depend on tumor location, symptoms, and risk of morbidity (e.g., Bektas 2023 and NCCN guidelines 1.2023). Until recently, surgery had
L72: been the mainstay of treatment; however, emerging data suggest a “wait and see” approach is reasonable for some patients. One of the most compelling pieces of data supporting observation for patients with DT comes from the published report of the placebo-controlled trial of sorafenib, in which the placebo arm had a 20% response rate, suggesting the importance of observation, especially in patients where symptoms are mild or absent (Gounder 2018). Surgery is offered to some patients who have potentially resectable extra-abdominal (extremity, trunk, breast) or abdominal wall DTs if they are medically able to tolerate surgery and if resection can be accomplished without major functional or cosmetic deficit. For the patients treated with surgery, recurrence rates have ranged from 19% to 77% with various factors affecting the rate of recurrence (Wang 2015). Primary radiation therapy (RT) is an appropriate option for patients who need local treatment but are not good surgical candidates, those who decline surgery, and those for whom surgical morbidity would be excessive. Systemic treatment is an alternative approach in these patients, especially if they are young and there are concerns about the potential for late toxicity from RT including but not limited to radiation-associated malignancies. Although a number of systemic therapies have activity against DTs there is no standard of care. There have been few prospective studies and most data from systemic treatment have come from case series or retrospective analyses (Gounder 2018). Systemic treatments described in the literature have included NSAIDS, hormonal therapy, conventional chemotherapy, and targeted therapy with tyrosine kinase inhibitors (e.g., Napolitano 2020 and NCCN v1.2023).
L73: The data submitted by the Applicant to support the efficacy of nirogacestat in patients with DT come from the NIR-DT-301 study, an international, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study in 142 adult patients with progressing DTs and forms the primary basis on which the Applicant is seeking approval of nirogacestat in the proposed indication. The major efficacy endpoint was progression-free survival assessed by BICR via RECIST v1.1. Median PFS in patients who received nirogacestat compared to placebo was non-evaluable (NE, 95% CI: NE, NE) for patients on nirogacestat and 15.1 months (95% CI: 8.4, NE) for patients on placebo with a hazard ratio (HR) of 0.29 (95% confidence interval [CI]: 0.15, 0.55], p &lt; 0.001). To support an overall favorable risk:benefit based on the effect on PFS, FDA conducted analyses on patient reported outcomes for improvement in pain, and results were consistent in showing that more patients in the nirogacestat arm experienced pain improvement over the placebo arm. Although a number of issues precluded FDA's ability to rely on the prespecified and tested PRO results, the overall results and the additional analyses for pain demonstrated that nirogacestat had an effect on pain in those who had pain at baseline.
L74: Safety data supporting the proposed indication reflected exposure to nirogacestat in patients enrolled on the NIR-DT-301 study (69 patients exposed to nirogacestat and 72 patients exposed to placebo), and additional supporting safety data from 17 patients
L75: from Study 14-C-0007 (an NCI-sponsored study), 9 patients from Study A8641014, and 19 patients from study A8641020 (refer to the clinical results section of this review for details on these supportive studies). The primary analysis of safety was based on 141 patients enrolled in the NIR-DT-301 study who received either nirogacestat or placebo. An important identified risk of nirogacestat is ovarian toxicity. Reports of primary ovarian toxicity (composite terms ovarian failure, premature menopause, amenorrhea, and menopause) were noted prior to the implementation of Amendment 3 (January 27, 2020) of NIR-DT-301. In Amendment 3, the Applicant included a requirement to ask women about their menstrual history and added monitoring of hormone levels in men and women. For women of childbearing potential experiencing one of the composite terms for ovarian toxicity, 75% experienced this adverse event (AE) versus 0% in the placebo arm. Although additional information was collected following Amendment 3 regarding ovarian toxicity, this information was not sufficient to fully characterize ovarian toxicity, resolution, and sequelae. In general, nirogacestat was well tolerated with most AEs reported as mild to moderate in severity. Diarrhea and gastrointestinal toxicity were the most common AEs. Diarrhea occurred in 84% of the safety population receiving nirogacestat versus 35% of patients receiving placebo. Diarrhea is a toxicity that oncologists are well-trained to manage and are acceptable for a population with a serious and life-threatening condition
L76: During the course of the review of the application, a major amendment was issued to the Applicant based in part on findings during the Office of Scientific Investigation (OSI). FDA inspection revealed discrepancies in the Applicant's reporting of sum of target tumor measurements for 7 patients; and deficiencies in the financial disclosure, and conflict of interest provided by the Applicant. Inspections of the clinical investigators revealed no discrepancies or regulatory violations. After a thorough review of the updated data, FDA did not find any significant discrepancies that would alter the positive risk:benefit for the treatment of patients with DT with nirogacestat.
L77: The review team recommends granting traditional approval to nirogacestat for the treatment of adult patients with progressing desmoid tumors who require systemic treatment.
L78: ## NDA/IND Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
## Nirogacestat (PF-03084014)
L79: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of Condition	DTs are rare, locally aggressive and invasive tumors, but do not metastasize.
DTs are diagnosed in approximately 900-1500 patients in the US annually.
Although extremity desmoids can also occur, most patients develop abdominal tumors, one-half of which are intra-abdominal, the remainder involving the abdominal wall leading to symptoms of pain and impaired mobility that can negatively impact their quality of life.
The 5-year survival of patients is dependent upon site of tumor, size, proximity to vital structures. For all patients with DT, nearly 96% of patients with DTs are alive 5 years after diagnosis, 92% after 10 years, and 87% after 15 years.
DT is a rare soft tissue sarcoma that is locally aggressive, causing significant morbidity and (less commonly) mortality and represents a population with a high unmet medical need that include early and accurate diagnosis and approved treatments.
Current Treatment Options	The are no FDA therapies approved for DT
Published treatment guidelines describe use of surgery, radiation, cytotoxic chemotherapy, or other targeted therapies.
Radiation may be an appropriate treatment but is reserved for patients who cannot tolerate systemic therapy and where surgery would be too morbid. Many factors play into the role of the use of surgery.
There is an unmet medical need for new effective systemic treatments for patients with DTs. There are no FDA-approved therapies. Due to the nature of desmoid tumors, complete surgical resection is often challenging and the risk of recurrence is variable and has been reported to be as high as 70%. Radiation therapy in combination with surgery may reduce the recurrence rate but given the high-rate of abdominal tumors, side effects may be a deterrent. For systemic therapies, there has been no consensus on standard of care.
L80: ## NDA/IND Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 217677
## Nirogacestat (PF-03084014)
L81: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Benefit	The mPFS in patients who received nirogacestat compared to placebo was non-evaluable (NE, 95% CI: NE, NE) and 15.1 months (95%CI: 8.4, NE), respectively. The HR was 0.29 (5% CI: 0.15, 0.55). The results were both clinically and statistically significant.
PRO data was collected during the conduct of the study to assess PRO measures from baseline over time. Due to missing data, FDA was not able to confirm the accuracy of PRO results submitted by the applicant, and therefore, the results were considered exploratory. A significant effect on patients is pain. Pain can be debilitating, require narcotic medication to properly control, and affect one's ability to perform daily functions of life. FDA performed additional analysis on the BPI short form and noted that nirogacestat had an effect on pain in those who had pain at baseline versus those who received placebo.
For progressing DTs who require systemic therapy there is no consensus on standard of care treatment for these patients in which the tumor may have debilitating effects on day to day living, significantly affecting their quality of life.
Improvements demonstrated in PFS and ORR were supported by favorable findings from patient-reported pain. Reducing a patient's pain is an important component in improving a patient's quality of life.
The results of the study meet the evidentiary standards for granting traditional approval in the proposed patient population.

Risk and Risk Management	Toxicity profile includes diarrhea and gastrointestinal toxicities, dermatological toxicities, and wasting of electrolytes (phosphorus and potassium). Toxicities were generally manageable with supportive care or dose interruption; dose modifications, and discontinuations.
Treatment with nirogacestat confers a risk of ovarian toxicity.
A PMR will be issued to capture appropriate data on women of child bearing potential to characterize ovarian toxicity in this patient population with potential labeling updates in the future for nirogacestat for ovarian toxicity.

Nirogacestat (PF-03084014)

L92: ## 2 THERAPEUTIC CONTEXT
L93: ## 2.1. Analysis of Condition
L94: ## The Applicant's Position:
L95: Desmoid tumors (DT), also referred to as aggressive fibromatosis (AF), are rare, locally invasive, slow growing soft tissue tumors that can cause severe pain and functional impairment by infiltrating or exerting mass effects on vital structures.
L96: DT arise in the deep soft tissue and are characterized by infiltrative growth and a tendency toward local recurrence (Kasper et al. 2011). DT cause significant morbidity by infiltrating or exerting mass effects on vital structures, which can lead to severe pain, functional impairment, and rarely, life-threatening complications (Lewis et al. 1999; Penel et al. 2017; Smith &amp; Kransdorf 2000). The most common primary tumor sites include abdominal walls, limbs, girdles, and mesenteric areas. Despite an inability to metastasize, they can behave aggressively, causing significant morbidity, with elevated rates of local recurrence following surgery (as high as 70%) despite wide excisions (Penel et al. 2017). Mortality is occasionally observed owing to the local aggressive nature and vital structure involvement of some DT (Smith &amp; Kransdorf 2000).
L97: DT most commonly occur in individuals between the ages of 15 to 60 years, with a peak age of about 30 years, and a 2- to 3-fold predominance in females (de Camargo et al. 2010; Skubitz 2017; Anneberg et al. 2022). The global incidence of DT is about 3 to 5 cases per million per year in the general population with approximately 1,000 to 1,650 cases diagnosed in the United States per year (van Broekhoven et al. 2015; Prevalence of Rare Diseases: Bibliographic Data, 2020).
L98: The incidence of DT is reported to be about 800- to 1000-fold higher in patients with familial adenomatous polyposis (FAP [Gardner Syndrome]), in which the adenomatous polyposis coli (APC) tumor suppressor gene is mutated (Skubitz 2017). FAP-associated DT is more frequently associated with abdominal tumors, especially in the Gardner variant of FAP, which is associated with intestinal polyposis, osteomas, fibromas, and epidermal inclusion cysts (Skubitz 2017). Intra-abdominal DT are one of the leading causes of death in patients with FAP (Quintini et al 2012). Although common in patients with FAP, most cases of DT occur spontaneously in adults and are associated with a mutation in \beta-catenin (*CTNNB1*) (Bo et al. 2012; Lazar et al. 2008; Tejpar et al. 1999). \beta-catenin is an integral component of the Wnt/\beta-catenin/T-cell transcription factor signaling pathway, which is frequently dysregulated in
L99: cancer. Patients with DT carrying \beta-catenin mutations have a lower 5-year recurrence-free survival rate than patients with wild-type tumors (Kummar et al. 2017).
L100: The clinical course of DT may be unusual and heterogeneous, characterized not only by tumor growth, proliferation, and disease progression, but also by stabilization and spontaneous remission (Desmoid Tumor Working Group [DTWG] 2020; Kasper et al. 2011). Compression of vital structures by DT can result in severe pain, functional impairment, nerve damage, and bowel obstruction or perforation (Constantinidou et al. 2012; Gounder et al. 2020; Hosalkar et al. 2006; Penel et al. 2017; Quintini et al. 2012; Shinagare et al. 2011; Skubitz 2017). Additionally, DT-specific morbidities can negatively impact school, work, and psychosocial functioning (Hosalkar et al. 2006; Schut et al. 2022).
L101: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L102: FDA agrees with the Applicant's assessment of DT. It is a rare tumor with an incidence of 2-4 cases per million per year in the United States (U.S.) and has been reported to comprise approximately 0.03% of all cancers (or tumors), and &lt;3% of all soft tissue sarcomas (Hosalkar et al., 2006). Ethnicity of 329 patients from the Desmoid Tumor Research Foundation Patient Registry noted the majority of patients with DT in the US to be 92% White, 1.6% Black or African American, 1.6% Asian, 0.3% American Indian or Alaskan native, and 5% other. DTs may occur during and after pregnancy, and regress during menopause, so hormones may play a role in the pathophysiology of DTs, and most DTs express low levels of nuclear estrogen receptor-\beta (Janinis et al., 2003). As stated by the Applicant, most DTs occur spontaneously, and approximately 85% are associated with a mutation in the CTNNB1 gene (most commonly in exon 3) (Skubitz et al., 2017). Approximately 10% of DTs are associated with an APC gene mutation, which is a tumor suppressor gene involved in \beta-catenin degradation. Five to 15% of DTs are associated with FAP (de Camargo et al., 2010; Skubitz et al., 2017). Somatic mutations in CTNNB1 and germline mutations in APC genes are probably mutually exclusive in DT (Penel et al., 2017). Although DT is rarely fatal, intra-abdominal tumors which account for 5% of spontaneous DTs are one of the leading causes of mortality. DTs associated with FAPs account for a small proportion of DTs, 80% are intra-abdominal, particularly in the Gardner variant of FAP (Quintini et al., 2012).
L103: ## 2.2. Analysis of Current Treatment Options
L104: There is no Food and Drug Administration (FDA)-approved therapeutic option for the treatment of DT, nor is there a universal standard of care for patients with advanced progressing tumors. Treatment options vary for each patient depending on the size, location, and morbidity associated with the tumor.
L105: Active surveillance is currently recommended as the first approach in DT (DTWG 2020). This approach includes continuous monitoring via magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) (or computed tomography [CT] if MRI is not feasible) within 1 to 2 months of diagnosis then at 3- to 6-month
L106: intervals. A decision regarding active treatment should be postponed until the occurrence of subsequent progression or increase of symptomatic burden, assessed with \ge 2 further assessments and possibly not before 1 year from diagnosis in the absence of fulfilling Response Evaluation Criteria in Solid Tumors (RECIST) progressive disease (PD; DTWG 2020).
L107: Historically, surgery was the therapeutic option of choice for localized, extra-abdominal, small volume DT; however, surgery is no longer regarded as the cornerstone of DT treatment given the associated morbidity and high rate of relapse after surgery (&gt; 70\%) (Penel et al. 2017).
L108: Radiotherapy has been used both in the adjuvant setting after surgery and in the primary setting, mainly for extra-abdominal tumors. Radiotherapy after surgery is an independent positive prognostic factor for local recurrence and overall survival, and radiotherapy alone or in combination with surgery led to significantly lower recurrence rates (Kasper et al. 2011).
L109: Various systemic therapies have been studied in patients with relapsed or recurrent DT, patients with DT that are not amenable to surgery or radiotherapy, or patients for whom surgery is potentially mutilating.
L110: Traditional chemotherapy may be considered in patients requiring systemic therapy for DT. Kasper et al. provided an overview of chemotherapy regimens that have been studied in patients with advanced disease (Kasper et al. 2011). A combination of methotrexate and vinblastine was one of the first chemotherapy regimens widely used for DT, with response rates ranging from 30% to 50%. Low-dose vinblastine-methotrexate combination has demonstrated disease stabilization and long-term tolerance in pediatric patients with progressive DT (Azzarelli et al. 2001; Fortunati et al. 2020; Penel et al. 2020; Skapec et al. 2007). Because this regimen does have considerable toxicity, vinorelbine has largely replaced vinblastine in this regimen. However, due to the toxicity of a prolonged course using either combination, this regimen is not well tolerated by adults long-term (Skubitz, 2017). It should be noted that these studies were small and uncontrolled, and the most effective chemotherapeutic regimen has not been established.
L111: As a standard-dose chemotherapy, anthracycline-based chemotherapy (doxorubicin plus dacarbazine) may be used in cases requiring rapid tumor response, e.g., intra-abdominal and head and neck tumors. The response rate is usually around 50% (Garbay et al. 2012), but it usually occurs as late as several months after the start of therapy. Shrinkage can continue even after treatment withdrawal and long-term disease control is achieved in up to 50% to 70% of the patients (DWTG, 2020).
L112: Tyrosine kinase inhibitors (TKIs) have been investigated and demonstrated some benefit in studies of adults with DT. Meaningful responses have been observed with TKIs, such as imatinib, sorafenib, and pazopanib (DTWG 2020; Gounder et al. 2018; Kasper et al. 2017; Toulmonde et al. 2019), although sorafenib is the only agent to have been studied in a
L113: randomized, double-blind (DB), placebo-controlled clinical trial in patients with DT and despite a positive Phase 3 study (hazard ratio [HR] for progression or death = 0.13; 95% CI, 0.05 to 0.31; p &lt; 0.001) in 87 patients with progressive, symptomatic, or recurrent DT, it is not FDA-approved for this indication (Gounder et al. 2018).
L114: A benefit in both tumor response and patient-reported outcomes (PROs) would be beneficial and clinically meaningful to patients with DT particularly given the significant unmet need as there are currently no FDA approved therapies for DT (DTWG 2020).
L115: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L116: DT are rare, locally-aggressive, and invasive soft-tissue tumors that can cause substantial morbidity including severe pain, functional impairment, and disfigurement affecting everyday activities. There is currently no accepted standard of care and no FDA approved therapies for the treatment of DT (DTWG, 2020; National Comprehensive Cancer Network, 2020). Nirogacestat has the potential to address a significant unmet medical need for this indication.
L117: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L118: FDA agrees with the Applicant's assessment of the current treatments offered in clinical practice for DTs. A multidisciplinary approach to diagnosis and treatment is warranted given the rarity and heterogeneous presentation of patients with DT.
L119: Due to the high recurrence rate after surgery as previously described by the Applicant, the DTWG 2020 consensus guidelines recommend initial active surveillance for patients who do not need immediate treatment (e.g., vital organ involvement, and intolerable symptoms). Data from retrospective studies are conflicting regarding whether adding post-operative radiotherapy decreases the recurrence rate, and because surgical management is less frequent in current practice, the combination of surgery and radiation is rarely done (DTWG 2020, NCCN 2.2023). Furthermore, a retrospective study in 142 patients with treatment-naïve (n=74) or previously treated (n=68) DT reported that 5-year progression-free survival was similar between patients who were underwent observation (47%) to patients who were treated with systemic therapy (54%, p=0.07); both patients with progressing and stable DTs were included in this retrospective study (Fiore et al., 2009). NCCN consensus guidelines state that surgery is the primary treatment for patients with resectable DTs, but that observation is an option for selected patients with resectable tumors. NCCN concludes that patients with DT "can be managed appropriately with a careful 'wait and see' approach if their tumors are asymptomatic and are not located in an area that could lead to functional limitations if the tumor increases in size" (NCCN 2.2023).
L120: For patients with DT who do need to initiate treatment, management is heterogeneous, but a more conservative approach with systemic therapy is preferred in some patients over local therapy, such as surgery or radiotherapy (DTWG 2020).
L121: ## 3 REGULATORY BACKGROUND
L122: ## 3.1. U.S. Regulatory Actions and Marketing History
L123: ## The Applicant's Position:
L124: Nirogacestat was studied under IND [REDACTED] (b) (4) by Pfizer. On 10 January 2018, Pfizer submitted a transfer of ownership for nirogacestat IND [REDACTED] (b) (4) to SpringWorks Therapeutics (SpringWorks). Per 21 Code of Federal Regulations (CFR) 312.50, SpringWorks submitted an acknowledgement of the sponsor transfer change for IND [REDACTED] (b) (4) on 19 January 2018.
L125: [REDACTED] (b) (4) SpringWorks then filed an administrative split from IND [REDACTED] (b) (4) for a new IND 138207 under the review jurisdiction of Division of Oncology 2 (DO2, formerly known as Division of Oncology Products 2 [DOP2]). The review jurisdiction for nirogacestat IND 138207 (DT) changed from DOP2 to DOP3 at the start of 2021. Refer to Table 1 for a summary of key regulatory activities.
L126: The following regulatory designations were granted to nirogacestat:
L127: - Orphan Drug: 07 June 2018
- Fast Track: 01 November 2018
- Breakthrough Therapy Designation (BTD): 28 August 2019
- Nirogacestat was also accepted into Real-Time Oncology Review (RTOR) by the Oncology Center of Excellence (OCE) (03 June 2022).
L128: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L129: FDA agrees with the Applicant's presentation of the U.S. FDA regulatory actions for nirogacestat. An initial Pediatric Study Plan (iPSP) was agreed upon between FDA and SpringWorks on April 25, 2022, for a partial waiver of PREA requirements for patients 0-&lt;2 years of age and a deferral in older pediatric patients awaiting the results of Children's Oncology Group (COG) Study ARST1921, which will be submitted post-approval. ARST1921 is an ongoing Phase 2 study evaluating the safety, pharmacokinetics, and efficacy of nirogacestat in pediatric patients &gt;1 to &lt;18 years-old with progressive, surgically unresectable DT. Study ARST1921 was activated in September 2020 and was expected to take approximately 3 years to accrue; per clinicaltrials.gov, updated on 11/22/2023, this study is active but no longer accruing patients. Patients are being followed for 2 years to evaluate outcomes including safety.
L130: Although Study ARST1921 allowed for the enrollment of patients above one year of age (rather than 2 years as described in the Waiver of the PREA requirement for patients ages 0&lt;2), FDA agreed with the waiver for children under age 2 years given the following:
L131: - Recent research and guidelines espousing a conservative strategy for desmoid-type fibromatosis in children (Orbach D et al. 2017, DTWG Guidelines 2020);
- COG study requirements for non-resectable tumors, progressing tumors, and previous systemic treatment;
- Observed risk of widening of growth plates, and theoretical risk on organ maturation (however, this risk would be solely based on non-clinical embryonic studies of the NOTCH pathway and not on data observed in patients); and
- Age distribution of 59 patients (over 63 months) in prior COG trial ARST0321 (Skapec SX, et al, 2013) with 78% of 59 patients being 10 years of age or older (additionally ARST0321 did not require previous systemic treatment).
L132: Given the above factors, enrollment of a sufficient number of patients younger than 24 months of age would be highly impracticable for this rare disease.

L154: ## 4 SIGNIFICANT ISSUES FROM OTHER REVIEW DISCIPLINES PERTINENT TO CLINICAL CONCLUSIONS ON EFFICACY AND SAFETY
L155: ## 4.1. Office of Scientific Investigations (OSI)
L156: Nirogacestat is a New Molecular Entity, so FDA inspected the Applicant (SpringWorks Therapeutics, Inc), the imaging contract research organization ( [REDACTED] (b) (4) ) and 3 domestic clinical sites that enrolled large number of patients and/or where most of the patients received the active drug, nirogacestat. The investigators at the clinical sites were Drs. Brian Van Tine (site #06), Mrinal Gounder (site #17), and Priscilla Merriam (site #22), and each site enrolled 5 to 14 patients. FDA has not previously inspected the Applicant or Dr. Merriam, and FDA had previously inspected [REDACTED] (b) (4) , and the other 2 investigators.
L157: FDA inspection revealed discrepancies in the Applicant's reporting of sum of target tumor measurements for 7 patients; and potential deficiencies in the financial disclosure, and conflict of interest provided by the Applicant. Inspections of the clinical investigators revealed no discrepancies or regulatory violations.
L158: FDA inspection of [REDACTED] (b) (4) revealed discrepancies for 5 patients in the sum of target tumor measurements between the blinded, independent reads and the data listings submitted to the FDA by the Applicant. The inspection revealed that the discrepancies occurred at the Applicant, prior to the submission to FDA. FDA asked the Applicant to address this discrepancy, and the Applicant initiated an internal investigation. The Applicant's internal investigation found 2 additional patients who had discrepancies in the sum of target tumors, and the Applicant determined that the root cause of discrepancies was a programming error by the CRO responsible for the statistical analysis and development of SDTM and ADaM datasets for Study NIR-DT-301 ( [REDACTED] (b) (4) ). The programming error added measurements of new lesions into the sum of target tumors, which error resulted in incorrect sum of target tumor measurements for 7 out of 142 patients enrolled in Study NIR-DT-301. The Applicant subsequently submitted a corrective and preventative action (CAPA) plan, as well as an Amendment to the CSR with the corrections in the sum of target tumor measurements for these 7 patients, and the associated efficacy tables, listings, figures, and datasets. FDA reviewed the datasets submitted on May 5, 2023, and was able to replicate the relevant tables and figures in the Amendment to the CSR.
L159: FDA inspection also observed deficiencies in financial disclosure and conflict of interest reporting by the Applicant. Three investigators [REDACTED] (b) (6) and were paid: [REDACTED] (b) (6) (site # [REDACTED] (b) (6) ), [REDACTED] (b) (6) (site # [REDACTED] (b) (6) ), and [REDACTED] (b) (6) (site # [REDACTED] (b) (6) ). However, only [REDACTED] (b) (6) disclosed that she received &gt;$25,000 on
L160: her financial disclosure form (FDF) and the other investigators listed no monetary stipend on their FDF. The Applicant reported no financial disclosures for any investigator or sub-investigator involved in Study NIR-DT-301 on their Form FDA 3454. [REDACTED] (site # [REDACTED]) disclosed [REDACTED] (receiving &lt;$25,000), and this service was not disclosed by the Applicant.
L161: FDA inspection also revealed that the Applicant did not have a procedure in place for evaluation of conflict of interest. The Applicant responded that they will consider expanding their existing financial disclosure procedure to include capturing conflict of interest and updating their financial disclosure process.
L162: In light of this report from OSI, the FDA clinical reviewer requested the Applicant to resubmit the financial disclosures forms and to provide the details of the disclosable financial arrangements or interest the Applicant had with clinical investigators. The Applicant responded that the financial disclosure forms they submitted were accurate with “no reportable financial conflicts of interest for all principal/sub-investigators (including [REDACTED] (b) (6) for Study NIR-DT-301.” The Applicant provided further details regarding [REDACTED] (b) (6) and [REDACTED] (b) (6) financial disclosures based on comments these investigators had provided in their financial disclosure forms. [REDACTED] (b) (6) which were independent of his work related to Study NIR-DT-301 and for which [REDACTED] (b) (6) was compensated &lt;$25,000. [REDACTED] (b) (6) also disclosed that [REDACTED] (b) (6) a monetary value of $25,000.” The Applicant states that [REDACTED] (b) (6).
L163: Based on the Applicant's response to FDA clinical reviewer's request regarding the financial disclosure, it appears that [REDACTED] (b) (6) and [REDACTED] (b) (6) received compensation for [REDACTED] (b) (6) that were not related to NIR-DT-301. [REDACTED] (b) (6) did receive compensation related to NIR-DT-301, i.e., compensation for [REDACTED] (b) (6), but the compensation was not more than $25,000. Thus, FDA agrees that the financial disclosure forms submitted by the Applicant were accurate and that the investigators did not have financial interests or arrangements as defined by 21CFR54.2. Furthermore, the financial arrangements are unlikely to impact the efficacy outcome of the trial given that the primary efficacy measure of NIR-DT-301 was determined by blinded, central review. Although there was a limited number of clinical progression events in the study, the overall results were supported by an analysis limited to an assessment of radiographs alone (as well as blinded assessment of ORR and PRO assessments).
L164: The Applicant also stated that they audited 8 clinical sites (including [REDACTED](b) (6)) during the trial and used data analytics software [REDACTED](b) (4) to programmatically perform surveillance for data irregularities.
L165: Based on the inspections, NIR-DT-301 appears to have been conducted adequately, and the data generated by the inspected clinical investigators and the imaging CRO appear adequate for review. Based on the review of the Amendment to CSR and datasets submitted to on May 5, 2023, the data submitted to support NIR-DT-301 are adequate for review.
L166: Lastly, errors in datasets that affected up to 16 tables and 6 figures for laboratory summary and shift tables, and one table for concomitant medications were identified. These updated, corrected tables and figures were received on May 5, 2023.

L173: ## 4.3. Clinical Microbiology
L174: The CMC review indicated that "production scale batches of nirogacestat hydrobromide drug substance were tested for TAMC, TYMC, and absence of E. Coli. The highest TAMC level observed was 20 CFU/g and no TYMC levels have been observed above 10 CFU/g. Batch history appears to indicate a low risk of microbial contamination or viability for the drug substance. This and the fact that the DS is not hygroscopic justifies no controls in the release specification for microbial limits."
L175: CMC also found that the microbial limit testing for DP at release and on stability to be acceptable. The microbiology laboratory at SGS France had been covered in a previous GMP inspection. Overall the Application was acceptable from a microbiological (manufacturing perspective).

L178: ## 5 NONCLINICAL PHARMACOLOGY/TOXICOLOGY
L179: ## 5.1. Executive Summary
L180: The established pharmacologic class of nirogacestat is gamma secretase inhibitor. By inhibiting the activity of gamma secretase, nirogacestat blocks the proteolytic activation of Notch, which has a role in cellular proliferation, differentiation, survival, and oncogenesis.
L181: Nirogacestat inhibited the activity of gamma secretase in vitro with an IC50 of 6.2 nM. In a cellular assay using human embryonic kidney (HEK) 293 cells that express a constitutively active NΔE mutant of Notch, nirogacestat inhibited the activity of Notch intracellular domain (NICD) with an IC50 of 7.7 nM. In human peripheral blood-acute lymphoid leukemia (HPB-ALL) cells that harbor Notch mutations, nirogacestat inhibited the activity of NICD with an IC50 of 41 nM and induced growth inhibition with an IC50 of 32 nM. Nirogacestat also demonstrated growth inhibition in various Notch expressing patient-derived desmoid tumor cell lines in vitro with IC50s of 4-160 µM. Nirogacestat exhibited anti-tumor activity in numerous Notch-driven tumor xenograft models in mice but did not induce tumor growth inhibition in a non-Notch-driven tumor xenograft model.
L182: SpringWorks conducted stand-alone GLP-compliant safety pharmacology studies in rats and dogs to assess the effect of nirogacestat on the central nervous system, pulmonary function, and the cardiovascular system. There were no nirogacestat-related adverse effects on the central nervous system, pulmonary function, or the cardiovascular system at single doses up to 500 mg/kg. Nirogacestat blocked the hERG channel with an IC50 of 1 µM; however, this IC50 is greater than 100 times the free Cmax in humans at the 150 mg BID dose. Consistent with these findings, nirogacestat did not have effects on QT prolongation clinically or in in vivo nonclinical studies.
L183: The toxicity of nirogacestat was assessed in GLP-compliant 3-month repeated-dose toxicity studies in rats and dogs. Shorter duration repeated-dose toxicity studies were also conducted with nirogacestat and the findings were generally consistent with 3-month repeated-dose toxicity studies. Rats received up to 25 mg/kg (50 mg/kg/day) oral nirogacestat, twice per day, for 3 months, resulting in mean exposure levels (AUC) up to 3.4 times the AUC of 6740 ng.h/mL in patients with desmoid tumors at the 150 mg BID dose. Dogs received up to 5 mg/kg (10 mg/kg/day) oral nirogacestat, twice per day, for 3 months, resulting in mean exposure levels (AUC) up to 0.46 times the AUC of 6740 ng.h/mL in patients with desmoid tumors at the 150 mg BID dose. Toxicity was generally consistent across species and consisted of intestinal
L184: hyperplasia, fibrinoid necrosis of blood vessels, lymphoid depletion, nephropathy, liver toxicity, and reproductive organ toxicity. Additional toxicities that only occurred in rats consisted of thickening of the hypertrophic cartilage zone, fibrosis/necrosis in the heart, and epidermal cysts. Male reproductive organ toxicity consisted of seminiferous tubule abnormalities, Sertoli cell degeneration, and epididymal oligospermia. Female reproductive organ toxicity consisted of ovarian degeneration/necrosis/atrophy, oocyte mineralization, and mammary acinar hyperplasia.
L185: In humans, nirogacestat can cause diarrhea, stomatitis, and ovarian dysfunction at the recommended 150 mg BID dose. Loose stool and intestinal hyperplasia occurred in rats and dogs administered nirogacestat. Intestinal hyperplasia was associated with epithelial inflammation and necrosis in dogs. Consistent with these adverse intestinal findings, Notch has been reported to regulate intestinal cell differentiation and inhibition of Notch drives goblet cell proliferation (Fre, et al., 2005; Milano, et al., 2004; Van Es, et al., 2005; Wong, et al., 2004). Notch also reportedly regulates gastric cell differentiation (Demitrack and Samuelson, 2016) which may contribute to stomatitis in humans. Regarding ovarian dysfunction in humans, ovarian degeneration/necrosis/atrophy and oocyte mineralization occurred in animals administered nirogacestat and was consistent with Notch regulation of mammalian folliculogenesis (Vanorny and Mayo, 2014).
L186: SpringWorks evaluated the effect of nirogacestat on male and female fertility in a dedicated GLP-compliant study. Male rats received up to 40 mg/kg/day of oral nirogacestat for 65 days including 28 days prior to mating with untreated female rats. Female rats received up to 40 mg/kg/day of oral nirogacestat for 35 days including 14 days prior to mating with untreated male rats and through Gestation Day 7. Nirogacestat resulted in reduced fertility when administered to male rats at \ge 5 mg/kg/day (approximately 0.16 times the 150 mg BID dose based on body surface area (BSA)) or female rats at \ge 20 mg/kg/day (approximately 0.65 times the 150 mg BID dose based on BSA). Nirogacestat resulted in a lack of fertility when administered to male or female rats at \ge 40 mg/kg/day (approximately 1.3 times the 150 mg BID dose based on BSA). In pregnant female rats, nirogacestat increased pre-implantation loss at \ge 20 mg/kg/day (approximately 0.65 times the 150 mg BID dose based on BSA).
L187: To address the potential for nirogacestat-mediated reproductive activity, SpringWorks conducted a preliminary embryo-fetal development study with nirogacestat in rats. Daily oral administration of nirogacestat to pregnant rats resulted in decreased fetal body weights, post-implantation loss, and fetal subcutis edema at doses \ge 20 mg/kg/day (approximately 0.85 times the exposure in patients with desmoid tumors based on AUC at the 150 mg BID dose). Based on
L188: these adverse findings, the team recommends a warning for embryo-fetal toxicity in Section 5 of the labeling. Because of the potential for embryo-fetal toxicity, a recommendation for females and males of reproductive potential to use contraception during treatment with nirogacestat and, based on a half-life of 25 hours, for 1 week after the last dose is included in Section 8.3 of the labeling. Lactating women are advised not to breastfeed during treatment with nirogacestat and, based on a half-life of 25 hours, for 1 week after the last dose.
L189: Nirogacestat was not considered genotoxic since it was not mutagenic in a bacterial reverse mutation (Ames) assay and was not clastogenic in an in vitro human chromosome aberration assay or in vivo rat bone marrow micronucleus assay.
L190: To support continuous daily oral dosing of nirogacestat, SpringWorks conducted a 26-week oral carcinogenicity study with nirogacestat in transgenic rasH2 mice. Mice received up to 100 mg/kg/day of oral nirogacestat, resulting in mean exposure levels (AUC) less than those in humans at the 150 mg BID dose. The results showed an increased incidence of whole body hemangiosarcomas in 100 mg/kg/day males that was above the historical control value for the test facility but did not reach statistical significance for trend and pairwise comparisons. The study was negative for drug-related neoplasms in female mice. Overall, the result of the transgenic mouse carcinogenicity study was concluded to be negative.
L191: Although activation of Notch signaling is associated with various cancers, literature reports that Notch signaling may paradoxically suppress tumorigenesis in certain tumor types including squamous cell carcinoma (SCC) and neuroendocrine tumors (Zhou, et al., 2022). Inhibition of Notch signaling has been associated with an increased incidence of skin cancers in humans (Coric, et al., 2012; Doody, et al., 2013; Penninkilampi, et al., 2016), and administration of nirogacestat to patients with desmoid tumors resulted in cutaneous squamous cell carcinoma and/or basal cell carcinoma in five patients, with no malignancies occurring in the placebo arm. Based on these adverse clinical findings, the clinical team recommends a warning and precaution for cutaneous malignancies in the nirogacestat label. No additional nonclinical studies to assess the tumorigenic potential of nirogacestat in animals are warranted based on the inclusion of the malignancy warning in the label.
L192: Nirogacestat is not expected to demonstrate phototoxicity given that it does not absorb light in the ultraviolet A and B (UVA-UVB)/visible range from 290 to 700 nm.
L193: There are no approvability issues from a pharmacology/toxicology perspective.
L194: ## 5.2. Referenced NDAs, BLAs, DMFs
L195: ## The Applicant's Position:
L196: There are no referenced NDAs, BLAs, or DMFs related to nonclinical pharmacology or toxicology for nirogacestat.
L197: ## 5.3. Pharmacology
L198: ## Primary Pharmacology
L199: ## The Applicant's Position:
L200: Nonclinical pharmacology characterized the effects of nirogacestat in cell-free assays, cell culture experiments, and animal models of human disease. Early nonclinical and clinical investigations were conducted with nirogacestat to evaluate potential therapeutic use in the treatment of Alzheimer's disease due to the involvement of gamma-secretase (GS) in the cleavage of the amyloid \beta peptide (A\beta); in Study A8641002
L201: Therefore, nirogacestat was evaluated in a variety of Notch-driven tumor models including T-cell acute lymphoblastic leukemia (T-ALL), breast cancer, colorectal cancer (CRC), pancreatic cancer, liver cancer, and prostate cancer. Results of early studies in participants with solid tumors demonstrated evidence of therapeutic activity in participants with DT, which led to the selection of this indication for further clinical development.
L202: ## In Vitro Pharmacology
L203: In vitro evaluation of enzyme kinetics was completed with cell-free assays using the human isoform of GS extracted from HeLa cells and a recombinant human APP-C100-FLAG peptide purified in *E. coli* (PF-03084014-Pharm-001). Cellular assays were also conducted using the human embryonic kidney (HEK) 293 stable cell line that expresses N\DeltaE construct (containing aa1727-2813 of NOTCH1). Nirogacestat displayed potent activity with IC_{50} of 7.7 nM (0.34 nM free) for NICD inhibition (PF-03084014-Pharm-001). In human peripheral blood-acute lymphoid leukemia (HPB-ALL) cells that harbor NOTCH1 mutations, the effects of nirogacestat on growth and NICD inhibition were assessed with the key biochemical and cellular properties provided in Table 2. For comparison to observed human serum values at steady state after a 150 mg BID dosing regimen, free mean maximum observed concentration (C_{max}) and minimum observed concentration (C_{min}) are estimated at 10.2 nM and 2.2 nM, respectively.
L204: **Table 2: Summary of Key Pharmacologic Properties of Nirogacestat**

The GS cleavage product A\beta1-40 was detected using a DELFIA-based immunoassay.
L208: b IC50 values were determined using NICD levels after exposure of HEK293 cells to nirogacestat. NICD was detected using a DELFIA-based immunoassay and are reported with correction for fu.
L209: c IC50 values were determined after exposure of HPB-ALL cells to serial dilutions of nirogacestat. IC50 for NICD was determined by Western blot analysis for cell lysates and IC50 for growth inhibition was determined using a resazurin assay.
L210: d IC50 values were determined by PK/PDx modeling using an indirect response model, with NICD as the PDx endpoint, and based on naïve pooling of individual observed PK and tumor PDx data. The model used data from HPB-ALL xenograft tumor models in mice and IC50 values are reported with correction for fu.
L211: ## Nirogacestat and Notch Signaling in Desmoid Tumors
L212: Nirogacestat was evaluated for antitumor effects in 7 patient-derived DT cell lines (Shang et al. 2015). *NOTCH1*, *JAG1*, and *HES1* were shown to be present in all patient lines, with high levels of nuclear localization of NICD confirming active Notch signaling. Dose-dependent decreases in NICD and *HES1* were confirmed by Western blot in 2 of the cell lines and a varying degree of growth inhibition was determined using cell proliferation assays in 5 of the cell lines at concentrations ranging from 0.01 to 10 \muM over a 27-day treatment period. For these patient-derived cells, the IC50 for growth inhibition ranged from 4.19 \muM in the most sensitive line to 158.8 \muM in the most resistant line. Cell migration and invasion assays were conducted with 5 of the patient-derived cell lines and all showed significant reductions in migration and invasion after 7 days treatment with 10 \muM nirogacestat. The IC50 values for growth inhibition
L213: and the migration reduction observed at 10 µM are higher than the total and free maximum serum concentrations observed at steady state in Study A8641014 (geometric mean: 2.54 µM and 10.2 nM, respectively). However, 4 of the 5 evaluated cell lines showed statistically significant growth inhibition at 1 µM, with 3 of the 5 showing statistically significant growth inhibition at 0.01 µM. Therefore, while the migration experiments may not conclusively support expected effects of nirogacestat at therapeutically relevant exposures, the effects on Notch-target expression and growth inhibition do support a role for GSI-mediated inhibition of Notch signaling as a potential mechanism for nirogacestat in the treatment of DT.
L214: ## In Vivo Pharmacology
L215: The antitumor activity of nirogacestat in a range of Notch-driven tumor models (T-ALL) and a non-Notch-driven tumor model (GTL-16) is provided in Table 3.

L222: ## Antitumor Efficacy and Association with NICD Inhibition After Oral Administration
L223: A series of experiments were performed with nirogacestat in human tumor xenograft models in athymic nu/nu mice to evaluate its ability to reduce NICD levels in tumors and establish a PK/PDx relationship for NICD reduction to plasma concentration of nirogacestat. In addition, the ability of nirogacestat to inhibit tumor growth or exhibit cytoreductive activity in mouse models and the relationship of antitumor efficacy to plasma concentration were also assessed.
L224: PK/PDx relationships for NICD inhibition were assessed exclusively in the HPB-ALL xenograft model due to the robust and consistent level of NICD inhibition observed in this model. To evaluate the NICD pharmacodynamic (PDx) response to nirogacestat, HPB-ALL tumors were harvested at several time points after oral administration at 2 dose levels (50 and 200 mg/kg) in single-dose studies. NICD levels in tumors were quantitated by Western blot analysis using an antibody against NICD. At 50 mg/kg, &gt; 50% reduction in NICD was observed by 4 hours and approximately 70% reduction in NICD was obtained at 24 hours postdose. The 200-mg/kg dose level was observed for up to 72 hours and achieved maximal NICD inhibition of approximately 80%, with the duration of NICD inhibition remaining greater than 40% for up to 48 hours. Similar results were also observed in the SupT1 T-ALL xenograft model.
L225: The HPB-ALL xenograft model was utilized to evaluate the relationship of TGI to time, dose-dependent inhibition of NICD, and plasma exposure of nirogacestat. To assess antitumor efficacy in HPB-ALL model, initial repeat dose studies were performed at 3 dose levels (17, 50, and 150 mg/kg BID for 14 days; PF-03084014-Pharm-001). Tumor volume was measured utilizing electronic Vernier calipers. Nirogacestat demonstrated robust antitumor activity in this model after 14 days of treatment in tumor-bearing mice (Figure 1A). Maximum TGI (&gt; 100%) by nirogacestat occurred at 150 mg/kg, with tumor regression observed in 6 out of 8 animals. A similar repeat dose study in the HPB-ALL xenograft using doses of 75 and 150 mg/kg BID was reported for up to 11 days (Wei et al. 2010). Maximal median reduction in tumor volume of approximately 75% and 92% was observed at the 75- and 150-mg/kg dose levels by Day 11, respectively (Figure 1B). Nirogacestat was well tolerated as no significant weight loss, morbidity, or mortality was observed at doses up to 100 mg/kg BID. At 150 mg/kg, slight body weight loss (10%–15%) was observed sporadically and this occurred usually after ~10 days of dosing. Dosing holidays were given to mice that lost &gt; 10% body weight until the body weight returned to normal. Additional immunohistochemical and gene expression assays in these studies supported the inhibition of Notch signaling and induction of apoptosis as associated effects of nirogacestat administration (Wei et al. 2010). Tumor samples at Day 7 of 150 mg/kg BID treatment showed significant reduction of NICD by Western blot relative to vehicle controls
L226: and notable reduction of expression in Notch-target genes *HES1* and *C-MYC* by reverse transcription polymerase chain reaction (RT-PCR) evaluation. Day 7 samples from the same treatment also exhibited a notable reduction of Ki67 staining and an increase in activated caspase-3 levels.
L227: **Figure 1:** Tumor Volume Reduction in Notch-driven HPB-ALL Xenograft Models
L228: The robustness of the HPB-ALL models suggests the mechanism of TGI is partly mediated through cell growth inhibition by nirogacestat. Tumor volume reduction was largely dose- and drug exposure-dependent with an exception at the lower dose levels (17 or 50 mg/kg). The exact reason for this observation is unclear although it is likely due to the high variability in tumor size of this model.
L229: ## **Key conclusions:**
L230: - At 150 mg/kg, inhibition of NICD between ~40%-80% was required to achieve complete inhibition of tumor growth (109% TGI at 150 mg/kg BID \times 14d).
- At 50 mg/kg, less NICD inhibition (20%-40%) was consistent with partial inhibition of tumor growth (43% TGI at 50 mg/kg BID \times 14d).
- Continuous NICD inhibition for the duration of the administration schedule at either dose level is necessary to achieve corresponding TGI.
L231: ## **PK/PDx Modeling**
L232: The PK/PDx data used for the modeling were based on 2 different studies with testing dose levels of 50 and 200 mg/kg over a 72-hour period (PF-03084014-Pharm-001). Based on this PK/PDx modeling, the plasma free IC50 for NICD inhibition in vivo was estimated at 0.42 nM, which is consistent with free cellular potency determined in HEK293 cells (Table 2). Single-dose PDx analysis revealed that the 50-mg/kg dose level yielded ~40%-50% NICD reduction, which correlates with ~50% TGI upon continuous BID dosing at this dose level. At 50 mg/kg, the free plasma area under the plasma concentration-time curve from 0 to 24 hours (AUC0-24) was 56
L233: ng·h/mL and free plasma steady-state average concentration (C_{avg}) was 2.3 ng/mL (4.8 nM). Taking into consideration protein binding differences between mouse and human, this target free plasma C_{avg} of 2.3 ng/mL equates to a total human plasma C_{avg} of 575 ng/mL, and this free plasma AUC_{0-24} of 56 ng·h/mL equates to a total human plasma AUC_{0-24} of 14,000 ng·h/mL. These predicted efficacious exposures are most comparable to the daily AUC of 12,860 ng·h/mL observed at steady state in participants receiving 150 mg BID in Study A8641014.
L234: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L235: FDA generally agrees with the Applicant's position. Note that the steady state AUC_{0-\tau} (where \tau=12hrs) in patients with desmoid tumors was 3370 ng·hr/mL after administration of nirogacestat at 150 mg BID.
L236: ## **Secondary Pharmacology**
L237: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L238: Nirogacestat was evaluated for interaction with a number of other proteases, receptors, ion channels, and kinases. Activity (IC_{50}) in a broad panel of common receptors was &gt; 1 \muM in all assays (757060). The IC_{50} was determined for all receptors where inhibition was greater than 50% at the screening concentration of 10 mM. Testing at other aspartic proteases (pepsin A, BACE 1 and cathepsin D) or serine proteases (chymotrypsin A, trypsin) showed negligible inhibition at 20 mM (PF-03084014-Pharm-001). Nirogacestat also showed less than 25% inhibition at 10 mM in a panel of 10 kinase enzymes, including ABL, CK1d, GSK3\beta, IKK2, IKKi, LCK, MK2, P38, PKA, PKCzeta (PF-03084014-Pharm-001). By comparison, the human serum free C_{max} observed at steady-state in Study A8641014 was 4.98 ng/mL (10.2 nM) after oral administration of 150 mg BID. Therefore, these off-target hits pose a minimal risk at the current therapeutic dose.
L239: ## **The FDA's Assessment:**
L240: FDA generally agrees with the Applicant's position; however, FDA notes the units in the Applicant's position that are in mM should be \muM based on the study report.
L241: ## **Safety Pharmacology**
L242: ## **The Applicant's Position:**
L243: The safety pharmacology of nirogacestat was evaluated in vitro and in vivo as outlined in Table 4. In vitro, nirogacestat and metabolite M283 (PF-03015273) were assessed for binding to the human ether-à-go-go related gene (hERG) to assess the risk of QT prolongation. The 1.0 \muM hERG IC_{50} is 98-fold over the human free geometric mean C_{max} of 4.98 ng/mL (10.2 nM) after administration of 150 mg BID as observed in Study A8641014. For the in vivo studies, a respiratory and neurobehavior study were conducted in rats, while a stand-alone
L244: cardiovascular study was conducted in telemetered dogs. There were no hazards identified in the safety pharmacology studies that would pose a risk in humans.

L250: ## 5.4. ADME/PK
L251: ## The Applicant's Position:
L252: The in vitro and in vivo nonclinical pharmacokinetic (PK) studies were designed to characterize the absorption, distribution, metabolism, and excretion (ADME) properties of nirogacestat. These studies enabled thorough evaluation of its pharmacological and toxicological profiles in preclinical animal models. Toxicokinetic (TK) data for nirogacestat were derived from Good
L253: Laboratory Practice (GLP) studies using liquid chromatography-tandem mass spectrometry (LC-MS/MS) methods validated under GLP conditions. Plasma protein binding and blood-to-plasma ratio were determined in mouse, rat, dog, and human. Brain penetration was evaluated in mouse and tissue distribution and excretion was evaluated in rat and dog. Initial in vitro metabolite profiling was performed across species to support selection of toxicology species. The in vitro cytochrome P450 (CYP) inhibition and induction potential of nirogacestat was also evaluated and nirogacestat was examined for effects on drug transporters in cell lines expressing specific hepatic and renal transporters to guide drug interaction risk assessments. Pharmacokinetic/pharmacodynamic (PK/PDx) modeling was performed on nirogacestat-mediated inhibition of NICD levels in tumors as a biomarker for inhibition of tumor growth. The ADME characterization of nirogacestat is sufficient to support the NDA.
L254: ## Absorption
L255: Nirogacestat exhibits moderate permeability in Madin-Darby canine kidney (MDCK) cells (19SPRW7), and at a pH range of 1.2 to 6.0 a 150 mg dose will be 100% dissolved; solubility decreases at pH \ge 6.5 (19SPRW12).
L256: PK parameters of nirogacestat following oral and intravenous (IV) administration of nirogacestat to male Sprague-Dawley rats (Report DM2006-03084014-018) and male and female Beagle dogs (Report DM2006-03084014-023) is provided in Table 5.
L257: **Table 5:** Mean (\pm StD) PK Parameter Values of Nirogacestat in Rats and Dogs Following Single-Dose Administration

L261: Absolute/Relative Bioavailability	In nonclinical species, rats and dogs, oral bioavailability was 3% and 15%, respectively.
Tmax	In toxicology studies, Tmax ranged from 0.5 to 7 hours.
L262: Distribution
Volume of Distribution	Estimates of apparent volume of distribution at steady state for nirogacestat were greater than total body water in rats and dogs, suggesting that nirogacestat readily distributes to tissues in these species.
Protein Binding	Initial plasma protein binding was reported in DM2006-03084014-020; however, the human plasma unbound fraction from that study was later found to be erroneously high. Serum protein binding was re-evaluated (191159, 135033, and 21SPRWP4) for mouse (CD1), rat (Sprague-Dawley), and dog (Beagle) with unbound free fractions of 0.2% for all three species. The fraction unbound of 0.4% was determined for human plasma and serum in confirmatory studies (2103037-03 and 21SPRWP2).
Tissue Distribution	A whole-body autoradiography study showed that radioactivity derived from [^{14}C] nirogacestat is widely distributed throughout the body as early as 30 minutes postdose (8299694). CNS exposure may be possible; however, radioactivity was primarily concentrated in non-circumventricular tissues. The extensive metabolism observed in mass-balance studies conducted in rat (8299694), dog (SPW/02), and human (NIR-DT-101 and NIR-DT-102) may also limit the interpretation of the autoradiography study with regard to tissue distribution of unchanged nirogacestat. A CNS distribution study (DM2006-03084014-017) showed that the brain-to-plasma AUC ratio was approximately 6-fold higher in mdr1a/1b(-/-) knockout mice compared with wild type, suggesting that P-gp efflux may serve to prevent accumulation of nirogacestat in the brain.
Metabolism
Metabolite Profile	In vitro metabolism of nirogacestat is consistent across nonclinical species and humans. All circulating metabolites observed in humans were present in microsomal incubations of one or more of the evaluated nonclinical species. In vitro, CYP3A4 was the major enzyme responsible for the metabolism of nirogacestat. This observation was supported by a healthy volunteer clinical DDI study (NIR-DT-103) where itraconazole-mediated CYP3A4 inhibition increased the serum AUC of nirogacestat by 8.23-fold. In vivo mass-balance studies in rat and dog both identified M283 (PF-03015273) as the most abundant major metabolite, though human studies (NIR-DT-101 and NIR-DT-102) found this metabolite to represent less than 10% of radioactivity recovered in serum. In all mass-balance studies, the largest fraction of recovered radioactivity is recovered in serum and was consistently unretained in high-performance liquid chromatography systems. As such, the resulting metabolites were not identified, but it is believed that they represent polar, low molecular weight compounds that are the result of extensive metabolism of the radiolabel into endogenous biomolecules.
CYP Induction and Inhibition	Nirogacestat induced CYP3A4, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, and CYP2C19 in human hepatocytes. Nirogacestat also reversibly inhibited CYP3A. Nirogacestat was determined to be a time-dependent inhibitor of CYP3A. Nirogacestat did not inhibit CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, and CYP2D6 and demonstrated little to no time-dependent inhibitory potency for these enzymes. In a healthy volunteer clinical DDI study (A8641002), the net
L263: effect of nirogacestat-mediated CYP3A4 interaction increased the serum AUC of midazolam by 58.9%.
Excretion
Transporters	
Nirogacestat is unlikely to be a substrate or inhibitor of MATE1, MATE2K, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT1, OAT3, or OCT2 at clinically relevant concentrations (092718 and 19SPRWP5).
Nirogacestat had an efflux ratio of 1.4 in BCRP-MDCK cells and is not likely to be a substrate of BCRP (14PFIZP1S1PF37P). Nirogacestat did not reach an IC50 for BCRP inhibition in MDCKII-LE cells at concentrations up to 120 µM (163120). Metabolite M283 (PF-03015273) exhibited poor permeability across MDR1-MDCK cells and was determined to be a substrate (efflux ratio 4.75) but not an inhibitor of P-gp (2003103 and 2003104).
An observed efflux ratio of 4.6 in MDR1-transfected MDCK cells suggests that nirogacestat is a substrate of P-gp (13PFIZP4S2-PF-8). Nirogacestat also inhibited P-gp-mediated efflux of digoxin in vitro with an IC50 of 2.3 µM. However, in a healthy volunteer clinical DDI study (NIR-DT-103), nirogacestat-mediated P-gp inhibition had no effect on the exposure of dabigatran etexilate.

Excretion Route	
In nonclinical studies, the primary route of excretion was the feces with more than 70% of the radiolabeled dose recovered in feces in both rats and dogs. In bile duct cannulated (BDC) rats, the primary elimination pathway following oral administration is hepatic clearance and biliary excretion with more than 46% of radioactivity recovered in the feces and between 28 to 31% excretion of radioactivity via biliary excretion.
Mean urinary excretion in mass-balance studies was 5.2% and 3.1% in rats and dogs, respectively. A mean of 7.0% of radiolabel was recovered in expired air in rats. In non-radiolabeled rat studies where nirogacestat was administered orally or intravenously, < 1% of unchanged nirogacestat was recovered in bile, urine, or feces.
L264: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L265: FDA generally agrees with the Applicant's position. Additional notable findings are described below.
L266: Tissue distribution: Excluding excreta, the highest Cmax levels of radiolabeled [14C] nirogacestat were uveal tract, liver, adrenal gland, spleen, and pituitary gland. Radiolabeled [14C] nirogacestat exhibited an affinity for pigmented ocular tissues containing melanin. Although low concentrations of [14C] nirogacestat-derived radioactivity were present in non-circumventricular central nervous system (CNS) tissues, radioactivity was higher in the choroid plexus than in the blood (approximately 7.5-fold higher AUC and 4.4-fold higher Cmax), which may suggest that nirogacestat crosses the blood-cerebrospinal fluid barrier. At 672 hours postdose, radioactivity concentrations persisted in thirteen tissues (adrenal gland, bone marrow, exorbital lacrimal gland, eyes, lymph nodes, meninges, pituitary gland, spleen, stomach mucosa, thymus, thyroid, uveal tract, and vitreous humor), most notably the uveal
L267: tract with concentrations that were at least 10-fold higher than those observed for all other non-ocular tissues.
L268: Metabolism: The percent contribution of the major cytochrome P450 (CYP) isoforms contributing to nirogacestat metabolism were CYP3A4 (85.7%), 2C9 (6.24%), 2C19 (10.5%), and 2D6 (5.03%).
L269: ## 5.5. Toxicology
L270: ## 5.5.1. General Toxicology
L271: ## The Applicant's Position:
L272: Nirogacestat was assessed in a series of nonclinical toxicology studies in alignment with applicable ICH guidance documents. The oral route of exposure was selected for these studies since the oral route is the intended clinical route of administration. The rat and dog were selected as the toxicology species based on nirogacestat PK and metabolism properties compared across species. The definitive studies were performed in the United States in accordance with the United States Department of Health and Human Services, FDA, United States Code of Federal Regulations, Title 21, Part 58: GLP for Nonclinical Laboratory Studies.
L273: Nirogacestat was administered to rats and dogs in repeat dose general toxicology studies up to 3 months in duration followed by a 1-month recovery. A 1-month mouse study was conducted to support a 6-month carcinogenicity study in transgenic mice. Many of the Nirogacestat-related toxicities were attributed to GS inhibition and disruption of the Notch signaling pathway. In the 1-month mouse study, target organs in males and females included the small intestine (duodenum, jejunum, and ileum), liver, femoral physis, sternal cartilage, thymus, and sex organs in both male and female mice. The no-observed-adverse effect-level (NOAEL) in the 1-month mouse study was 20 mg/kg/day. In the 3-month rat study, ovarian atrophy, alterations in the estrous cycle and decreased cellularity in gut-associated lymphoid tissue (GALT) in females, and decreased cellularity of mesenteric lymph nodes in males and females at 5 mg/kg/day was observed. A NOAEL was not identified in this 3-month oral toxicity study in rats due to these effects. In addition, all dose levels evaluated in the rat study showed chronic progressive nephropathy, pulmonary phospholipidosis, and salivary gland necrosis in a dose-dependent manner. In the dog study, treatment-related effects were present within the intestines, spleen, gall bladder, liver, kidney, testes, and ovary. The intestinal and liver findings were associated with generalized inflammation and associated clinical pathology changes in most of these animals. In the recovery dogs, only the intestinal, testicular, and ovarian findings were persistent but at lower severity, suggesting evidence of reversibility. Due to oocyte mineralization at the lowest dose in the dog study, a NOAEL was not identified.
L274: The exposures at the NOAEL or lowest observed adverse effect level in the 1 and 3-month studies were below the human steady state exposures after administration of nirogacestat at 150 mg BID. Conduct of additional general toxicology studies were not required based on FDA feedback received at the end-of-Phase 1 meeting; therefore, the 6-month rat and 9-month dog studies with nirogacestat were not conducted. See Table 14 for safety margin calculations.

Findings
Died or Sacrificed
Moribund	Moribund animals were euthanized early on Days 84 and 89 in the high dose females. Due to mortality and adverse clinical signs in female rats at the 50-mg/kg/day dose level, dose administration was terminated for female rats at this dose level after 88 consecutive days. Two remaining female toxicokinetic animals at this dose level were incorporated into the main study on Day 88 and a cohort of 50-mg/kg/day female rats were necropsied on Day 89, while the remaining female rats in this dose level continued on study to assess recovery.
Body Weight and
Food Consumption	On Day 64 for male rats and Day 57 for female rats, decreases in mean body weight gain were observed at 50 mg/kg/day, secondary to significant decreases in food consumption in most female rats.
No Noteworthy
Findings	Ophthalmology and urinalysis
Clinical
Observations	Limited to the high dose group and consisted of decreased activity, hunched posture, pallor, rough and stained fur, and chromodacryorrhea.
Hematology and
Flow Cytometry	Decrease red blood cell parameters and lymphocytes at ≥ 20 mg/kg/day; increases in neutrophils at ≥ 20 mg/kg/day. Decreases in all T-cell subsets and decreased IgD expression on B cells, and increased NK cells at ≥ 20 mg/kg/day.
Clinical Chemistry	Increased alanine aminotransferase, aspartate aminotransferase, alkaline phosphatase, gamma-glutamyl transferase, blood urea nitrogen, creatinine, and total bilirubin in females at 50 mg/kg/day. Slight increases in alanine aminotransferase, aspartate aminotransferase, and cholesterol were elevated in mid-dose females and high dose males.
L296: Organ Weight	Meaningful organ to body weight ratio changes compared to controls included increases in kidney and liver weights; decreases in thymus and ovarian weights at \ge 20 mg/kg/day.

Lung foamy macrophage at \ge 5 mg/kg/day
Hepatocellular hypertrophy at \ge 5 mg/kg/day
Progressive nephropathy in male rats at \ge 5 mg/kg/day
Hyperplasia of the small intestinal epithelium
Adrenal vacuolation and necrosis at \ge 20 mg/kg/day
Myonecrosis/fibrosis in the heart in females \ge 20 mg/kg/day and 2 male rats at 50 mg/kg/day

Post-Dose Observations	During the recovery phase, decreases in mean body weight gain were observed at \ge 20 mg/kg/day. Increases in food consumption were observed in female rats at 50 mg/kg/day during the recovery phase. Increased metaphyseal bone and epidermal cysts in male and female rats, cardiomyopathy in male rats, and ovarian follicular cysts, disruption of the estrous cycle, decreased ovarian weights, increased liver weights, myonecrosis/myofibrosis, and hyperplasia of the mammary gland in female rats were still present after the recovery period.

L323: ## 5.5.2. Genetic Toxicology
L324: ## The Applicant's Position:
L325: The genotoxicity of nirogacestat was assessed in a battery of genotoxicity studies in alignment with ICH S2(R1) guidance. Nirogacestat was assessed in vitro in the bacterial mutagenicity assay (O6GR106), the in vitro cytogenetic (human lymphocyte) assay (O6GR107), and in vivo in a rat micronucleus study (01214020). These in vitro tests were conducted with and without exogenous metabolic activation using concentrations up to those limited by cytotoxicity or insolubility, while in vivo nirogacestat was dosed up to the limit dose of 2000 mg/kg/day.
L326: Nirogacestat was negative in both in vitro assays, as well as the in vivo micronucleus study as summarized in Table 11.
