L335: ## 5.5.3. Carcinogenicity
L336: ## The Applicant's Position:
L337: The carcinogenicity of nirogacestat was assessed in a 6-month repeat dose study in transgenic rasH2 mice (20174162). Nirogacestat was administered orally BID (approximately 10 hours apart) for a minimum of 26 weeks to cByB6F1/Tg rasH2 hemizygous mice at dose levels of 5, 15, and 50 mg/kg/dose (10, 30, and 100 mg/kg/day).
L338: Survival at 10 or 30 mg/kg/day nirogacestat ranged from 92% to 96% in males and from 96 to 100% in females and was comparable to controls, whereas survival at 100 mg/kg/day was reduced to 76% in males and females at study termination. Although there were no statistically significant effects on mortality when pairwise comparisons were made between the control group and 100 mg/kg/day males or females, there was a statistically significant dose-related trend of increased mortality in the females.
L339: Administration of nirogacestat BID by oral gavage to Tg RasH2 mice for 26 weeks at 10 or 30 mg/kg/day resulted in no effects on survival or tumor formation. At 100 mg/kg/day, administration of nirogacestat resulted in an increase in the frequency of spontaneous hemangiosarcoma in males and females. There were no significant increases in new neoplasms associated with nirogacestat administration at doses up to 100 mg/kg/day. The systemic exposures in mice at this dose level are below those in humans administered nirogacestat at 150 mg BID.
L340: Based on the Agency's feedback for Study 20174162 provided by email on 11 January 2022, SpringWorks submitted an updated waiver request for its proposal that a 2-year rat carcinogenicity study was not required. As requested by the Agency, the updated justification addressed duration and exposure, carcinogenic potential, genotoxicity, indication and participant population, general toxicology study findings (e.g., histopathology, findings from the 26-week mouse carcinogenicity study, and other relevant factors). See Table 14 for safety margin calculations.

L350: ## 5.5.4. Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
L351: ## The Applicant's Position:
L352: The developmental and reproductive toxicology assessment of nirogacestat consisted of two separate fertility studies in male and female rats and a preliminary embryo-fetal developmental (EFD) toxicity study in pregnant rats. The first fertility study was conducted by simultaneous treatment of male and female rats before mating (01214013). The second fertility study was conducted by treating male rats with nirogacestat then mating with untreated female rats, along with treated female rats mated with untreated known breeding male rats (01214026). Only Study 0124026 is tabulated below.
L353: Nirogacestat produced embryo toxicity when administered to pregnant rats, and impaired fertility in both male and female rats. In the EFD study in rats, nirogacestat induced significant embryo loss, early resorptions, and decreased fetal weights in surviving embryos. These negative effects on embryonic development were anticipated based on transgenic studies in mice demonstrating that the loss of Notch signaling is embryonically lethal (Donoviel et al. 1999; Swiatek et al. 1994). The changes in sex organs in nirogacestat-treated male and female rats were also anticipated based on the known role of the Notch pathway in the ovary and testes. The ovarian changes in rats and dogs, along with altered estrous cyclicity, are likely due to inhibition of Notch signaling in ovaries, as this signaling pathway is critical in the regulation of mammalian folliculogenesis (Vanorny et al. 2017). Similar ovarian changes were observed with another GS inhibitor (Simutis et al. 2018). In the testes, the Notch signaling is critical for spermatogenesis (Murta et al. 2016). These effects on both male and female sex organs could explain the effects of nirogacestat on fertility indices measured in both male and female rats.
L354: Based on the severity of the embryo-fetal toxicities, and the known requirement for the Notch pathway for normal embryo implantation and development, the weight of evidence provides justification for not conducting the definitive embryo-fetal toxicity studies in rats and in rabbits, as well as not conducting the rat peri- and post-natal development study. As shown by Donoviel et al (1999), mice lacking Notch activity by inactivation of presenilin 1 and 2 (upstream of Notch and required for GS activity), leads to perinatal lethality due to multiple early developmental effects. This finding supports earlier studies in mice where a loss of Notch1 is embryonically lethal (Swiatek et al, 1994). In addition, incubating zebrafish embryos with a GS inhibitor led to impaired somite segmentation (Geling et al 2002), which is similar to other lethal effects seen in the embryos of mice with altered Notch activity. The embryonic loss in the
L355: rat embryo-fetal development study, and the early embryonic loss in the fertility studies align with the known requirement of Notch for embryonic development. In addition, the exposures at which these effects occur in rat are below (\le 0.1-fold) the human systemic exposure based on AUC (12860 ng\cdothr/mL) after administration of 150 mg of nirogacestat BID. See Table 14 for safety margin calculations.

L385: ## 5.5.8. Safety Margins
L386: ## The Applicant's Position:
L387: The calculated safety margins for the definitive repeat dose toxicity studies with nirogacestat were calculated by dividing the total AUC_{0-24} from the respective toxicology study at the NOEL or LOEL with the human total steady state AUC_{0-24} (12860 ng·hr/mL) dosed at 150 mg BID (Table 14). The margins were based on exposures that were available at the end of the dosing period for the toxicity study. Because of the sex difference in rats, the margins were determined for both sexes, while for dogs and mice, margins were based on sex combined group mean values. Total exposures were used given that nirogacestat is highly protein bound in mouse, rat, dog and human plasma with fraction unbound of 0.2%, 0.2%, 0.2%, and 0.4%, respectively.

The human exposure margins in Table 14 were calculated using a total human steady state AUC of 12860 ng·hr/mL after administration of nirogacestat at 150 mg BID. Note that the steady state AUC0-tau (based on a dosing interval of 12 hours) in patients with desmoid tumors was 3370 ng·hr/mL (or approximately 6740 ng·hr/mL for comparison with animal AUC0-24 data) after administration of nirogacestat at 150 mg BID.

L404: ## 6 CLINICAL PHARMACOLOGY
L405: ## 6.1. Executive Summary
L406: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L407: Nirogacestat is a gamma secretase (GS) inhibitor that blocks proteolytic activation of the Notch receptor. When dysregulated, Notch can activate pathways that contribute to tumor growth. The proposed recommended dosage of nirogacestat is 150 mg BID with or without food.
L408: The clinical pharmacology review focused on the evaluation of appropriateness of the proposed dosage, administration of nirogacestat with food, use in patients with hepatic impairment (HI), and potential drug-drug interactions (DDI).
L409: The proposed dosage of nirogacestat 150 mg BID with dose reduction to 100 mg BID for selected adverse reactions is generally acceptable based on the integrated analysis of PK, PD, efficacy and safety. The clinical effectiveness was demonstrated in pivotal Study NIR-DT-301 (*See Section 8 for details*). The overall safety of nirogacestat is considered acceptable and clinically manageable with a relative high dose intensity (87%). In addition, exposure-response analysis supported that patients who underwent dose reductions had similar best overall response (BOR) rate and duration of response (DoR) than those without dose reduction.
L410: The review team agrees with the Applicant's proposal of administering nirogacestat without regard to food as the sub-study of food effect in the EXPAND-S phase of Study A8641014 showed that a high-fat meal had no clinically significant effect on nirogacestat exposure.
L411: The review team assesses that no contraindication of nirogacestat for hepatic impairment (HI) is necessary based on the clinical AE evaluation on liver toxicity and the results from the HI study. The review team recommends that no dosage adjustment is necessary in patients with mild or moderate HI as the change in nirogacestat exposure in patients with moderate HI is not considered clinically meaningful compared to healthy subjects in the HI study NIR-DT-104. Insufficient data are available to characterize nirogacestat PK in patients with severe HI as
L412: clinical data was only available in one patient with severe HI. However, the review team also does not recommend a dosage adjustment in patients with severe HI.
L413: The review team recommends a PMC to conduct a clinical PK study to assess the effect of a proton pump inhibitor (PPI) on the PK of a single dose of nirogacestat as the in vitro study demonstrated that the solubility of nirogacestat was reduced by more than 87% at pH values of 6.0 and above. However, the effect of coadministration of acid-reducing agents (ARAs) with nirogacestat on its PK, efficacy, and safety has not been characterized in clinical studies. In addition, the review team recommends that concomitant use of PPIs and H2 receptor antagonists should be avoided, and that administration of antacids be staggered relative to the administration of nirogacestat.
L414: The review team agrees with the Applicant's position that (1) the concomitant use of nirogacestat with strong or moderate CYP3A inhibitors and inducers should be avoided based on the results of the dedicated DDI study NIR-DT-103 and PBPK simulations and (2) the concomitant use with certain CYP3A4 and CYP2C19 substrates should be avoided where minimal concentration changes may lead to serious adverse reactions (CYP3A4 substrates) or significant decreases in efficacy (CYP2C19 substrates) based on the PBPK simulations.
L415: ## 6.1.1 Recommendations:
L416: This NDA is approvable from a clinical pharmacology perspective. The key review issues with specific recommendations and comments are summarized in Table 15 below.
L417: **Table 15: Key Review Issues with Specific Recommendations and Comments**
L418: Review Issue	Recommendations and Comments
Pivotal and supportive evidence of effectiveness	Study NIR-DT-301 showed a statistically significant improvement in PFS for nirogacestat 150 mg BID compared to that of placebo. The clinical pharmacology (e.g., exposure-response analyses for efficacy, tumor size and safety) program provides supportive evidence of effectiveness.
General dosing instructions	The proposed recommended dosage of 150 mg BID taken with or without food is acceptable based on the following:This proposed dosage has been demonstrated to be efficacious in the pivotal Study NIR-DT-301.
The safety profile of nirogacestat is considered acceptable and manageable.
Administration of nirogacestat without regard to food is supported by the results of the sub-study of food effect in
L419: Study A8641014 showing that a high-fat meal had no clinically significant effect on nirogacestat exposure.
Dosing in patient subgroups (intrinsic and extrinsic factors)	
Based on clinical PK data, PBPK, and exposure-response analyses, the following dosage adjustments are recommended:
Avoid concomitant use with strong and moderate CYP3A inhibitors and inducers.
Avoid concomitant use with PPIs and H2 receptor antagonists. Stagger administration with that of antacids.
Avoid concomitant use with certain CYP3A4 and CYP2C19 substrates where minimal concentration changes may lead to serious adverse reactions.
No dosage adjustment is recommended for patients with mild, moderate, or severe HI. Insufficient data are available to characterize nirogacestat PK in patients with severe HI.
No dosage adjustment is recommended for patients with renal impairment (RI).

Bioavailability between the uncoated and film-coated tablets used in clinical studies	
Relative BA study between uncoated and film-coated tablet (to-be-market formulation) is not necessary as the dissolution profiles between uncoated and film-coated tablets are nearly identical at pH conditions of 1.2, 4.5 and 6.8. Refer to CMC review for additional details.

Labeling	
Following are the key changes to the Applicant's proposed labeling:
(b) (4)
Concomitant use of PPIs and H2 receptor antagonists should be avoided and the administration of antacids should be staggered relative to the administration of nirogacestat
PK parameters and characteristics of nirogacestat in Table 6 are updated.
L420: ## 6.1.2 Post-Marketing Requirements and Commitments
L421: A PMC is proposed to evaluate if proton pump inhibitors alter the bioavailability of nirogacestat and to determine appropriate dosing recommendations for nirogacestat with regard to use of concomitant gastric acid reducing agents. Refer to Section below describing PMCs/PMRs.
L422: ## 6.2. Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Assessment
L423: ## 6.2.1. Pharmacology and Clinical Pharmacokinetics
L424: ## Data:
L425: Nirogacestat PK has been studied in 6 completed Phase 1 clinical studies conducted in healthy adult human participants (Studies A8641001, A8641002, A8641008, NIR-DT-101, NIR-DT-102, and NIR-DT-103). An additional Phase 1 study (NIR-DT-104) in participants with hepatic impairment is ongoing; an interim PK analysis has been completed and included in this review. In these studies, single oral doses of nirogacestat up to 150 mg and multiple doses up to 95 mg (Study A8641002) QD for up to 14 days have been administered. Nirogacestat was studied in participants with advanced cancers as a monotherapy in Study A8641014 utilizing doses ranging from 20 mg to 330 mg twice daily (BID), continuously. Additional investigations as monotherapy and in combination with other agents in breast cancer and pancreatic cancer were conducted; however, data were limited with regard to understanding the PK and PDx of nirogacestat. In addition, PK samples were collected in a Phase 3 study (Study NIR-DT-301; see Section 8.1.1) that was conducted using nirogacestat 150 mg BID in adult participants with progressing DT/AF. A pediatric study sponsored by the Children's Oncology Group (COG) is currently ongoing in participants aged &gt; 12 months to &lt; 18 years of age; data from this ongoing study is not included in this NDA.
L426: Evaluation of the nirogacestat PK characteristics in healthy participants and participants with cancer diagnoses indicated that the compound was rapidly absorbed, with median time of occurrence of maximum serum concentration (C_{max}) (T_{max}) values of 0.5 to 1 hour generally observed. Nirogacestat exposure increased with escalating single and repeat doses, with greater than dose proportional increases observed across the dose range evaluated in single and multiple ascending dose studies. The terminal elimination half-life (t_{1/2}) of nirogacestat was approximately 19 hours after single doses and 26 hours after multiple doses. Nirogacestat is highly bound to both proteins, but with a greater affinity for serum human \alpha1-acid glycoprotein (AAG) than for serum albumin. In vitro studies indicated that nirogacestat is primarily metabolized by cytochrome P450 3A4 (CYP3A4). The impact of CYP3A4 metabolism on nirogacestat bioavailability and elimination was confirmed in a drug interaction study with itraconazole, leading to the conclusion that nirogacestat is a sensitive substrate for CYP3A4.
L427: Two human mass balance studies (NIR-DT-101 and NIR-DT-102) were conducted to examine routes of excretion and identify potentially active metabolites. The primary route of excretion was through feces and very little unchanged nirogacestat recovered in feces, urine, or expired air. No major circulating metabolites were identified.
L428: The PK of nirogacestat in pediatric populations has not been fully evaluated at this time. An Agreed Initial Pediatric Study Plan (iPSP) is in place to inform use in children. Studies have not been performed specifically in geriatric populations; however, age was a covariate in the Population PK (PopPK) model and no significant effect of age was found on nirogacestat PK. Because renal elimination is not the primary clearance pathway, studies have not been performed for a renally impaired population. A study is currently ongoing to evaluate nirogacestat in participants with hepatic impairment (Study-NIR-DT-104); interim PK data from this study establish that the PK of nirogacestat is altered by hepatic function. Specifically, participants that met the moderate impairment criteria by Child-Pugh but did not have total bilirubin levels &gt; 1.5 \times upper limit of normal (ULN) (National Cancer Institute – Organ Dysfunction Working Group [NCI-ODWG] moderate severity Group C) did not show significantly altered nirogacestat PK. Therefore, it is reasonable to conclude that patients with mild or moderate hepatic impairment and total bilirubin levels \le 1.5 \times ULN (NCI-ODWG normal or mild) can be treated with nirogacestat without dose modification.
L429: Study A8641014 evaluated participants for a food effect on PK and reported that the geometric means were similar in the fed versus fasted condition for both C_{max} and the area under the plasma concentration-time curve over the dosing interval (AUC_{\tau}); however, significant variability was observed for both PK parameters. Given the observed efficacy and safety parameters from Study NIR-DT-301 combined with reasonable variability from the food effect assessment in Study A8641014 relative to other assessments of nirogacestat PK, nirogacestat is recommended for administration without regard to food.
L430: Nirogacestat also demonstrated evidence for both reversible and time-dependent inhibition of CYP3A4. A clinical drug interaction study (NIR-DT-103) evaluating the effects of nirogacestat on midazolam identified a weak inhibition effect on CYP3A4. In vitro studies indicated that nirogacestat inhibited the efflux transporter P-glycoprotein (P-gp) at clinically relevant concentrations; however, exposure of total dabigatran was not affected by nirogacestat administration in a clinical drug interaction study, supporting the absence of clinically meaningful P-gp inhibition by nirogacestat. A physiologically based pharmacokinetic model (PBPK) was developed based on in vitro and clinical data for nirogacestat and the effects of CYP3A induction on nirogacestat, as well as potential for nirogacestat to induce several CYP enzymes, were predicted using this model. Nirogacestat was predicted to have a potential
L431: induction effect on CYP2C19 in a sensitivity analysis using ^{(b)}_{(4)} times the maximum induction rate (IndMax) identified through in vitro evaluations; however, a minimal effect was predicted at the IndMax. The PBPK model was also applied to simulate the effects of various dose modification strategies in the event of a need to prescribe strong or moderate inhibitors of CYP3A4.
L432: A population PK (PopPK) analysis was conducted using studies from 6 healthy participant studies (A8641001, A8641002, A8641008, NIR-DT-101, NIR-DT-102, and NIR-DT-103), a Phase 1 study in participants with advanced solid tumors (A8641014), and the Phase 3 study in participants with DT (NIR-DT-301). A three-compartment structural model, with two peripheral distribution compartments, first-order absorption with lag time, and central clearance was selected to describe nirogacestat PK. Notable covariates included itraconazole administration on bioavailability and clearance, participant population on clearance, and weight on the volume of distribution for the 2 peripheral compartments. Clearance was reduced approximately 44% in the advanced solid tumor and T-ALL/lymphoblastic lymphoma (LBL) population relative to healthy participants and participants with DT, suggesting that PK in participants with DT is more similar to healthy participants than participants with advanced malignancies.
L433: ## The Applicant's Position:
L434: Nirogacestat is rapidly absorbed, highly protein bound, and extensively metabolized by CYP3A4. No major circulating metabolites with activity against GS are known to occur and very little unchanged nirogacestat is recovered in feces, urine, or expired air. A PopPK analysis using several healthy participant and participant studies found that nirogacestat PK in participants with DT is more similar to PK observed in healthy participants than to PK observed in participants with advanced malignancies. Given the observed efficacy and safety parameters from Study NIR-DT-301 combined with reasonable variability from the food effect assessment in Study A8641014 relative to other assessments of nirogacestat PK, nirogacestat is recommended for administration without regard to food. The clinical PK of nirogacestat has been well characterized.
L435: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L436: FDA generally agrees with the Applicant's assessment of the available PK data. However, FDA notes that there is a significant difference in PK of nirogacestat administered to patients with DT and nirogacestat administered to patients with other solid tumor malignancies. Thus, the listed PK parameters such as, C_{max}, AUC_{tau}, and terminal elimination half-life of nirogacestat at steady state following 150 mg BID in patients from Study A8641014 may not be representative of patients with DT. Section 12 in the nirogacestat USPI reflects PK data from patients with DT. FDA agrees with the Applicant's position that the concomitant use of nirogacestat with strong or moderate CYP3A inhibitors and inducers should be avoided. FDA also agrees with the
L437: Applicant's position that concomitant use with certain CYP3A4 and CYP2C19 substrates should be avoided where minimal concentration changes may lead to serious adverse reactions.
L438: FDA's assessment in Sections 6.3.1 and 6.3.2.4 below for details.
L439: (See Therapeutic Individualization Section below).
L440: FDA verified the population PK model and agreed with the Applicant's position that the population PK model described the observed data reasonably well and was considered adequate to predict individual PK estimates for subsequent PK/PD and E-R analysis.
L441: ## 6.2.2. General Dosing and Therapeutic Individualization
L442: ## 6.2.3. General Dosing
L443: ## Applicant's Position:
L444: The selection of 150 mg BID as the RP2D was made using the totality of evidence from Study A8641014, a Phase 1 dose-finding study of nirogacestat in participants with advanced solid tumors (see Section 8.1.7). This included the identification of the maximum tolerated dose (MTD) at 220 mg BID and data from PK and PDx measurements, as well as review of objective responses among a broad range of solid tumor types. The high response rate in the participants with DT (objective response rate [ORR]: 71.4%) led to the initiation of a Phase 2 investigator-initiated study that was conducted at 150 mg BID (Study 14-C-0007; Kummar et al. 2017). The rate of response for participants with DT in these two studies (ORR: 71.4% [Study A8641014] and 31% [Study 14-C-0007]), and the relative tolerability of the 150 mg BID dose level compared to the 220 mg BID dose level were primary factors in selection of the 150 mg BID dose in Study NIR-DT-301. For selected toxicities, dosing was interrupted and/or reduced to 100 mg BID.
L445: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L446: See FDA's assessment in the section 6.3.2.2 below for details.
L447: ## 6.2.4. Therapeutic Individualization
L448: ## The Applicant's Position:
L449: As described in Section 6.2.3, dosing will be interrupted and/or reduced to 100 mg BID for patients who experience selected toxicities or for patients who experience life-threatening adverse reactions.
L450: Nirogacestat PK has been characterized based on extrinsic and intrinsic factors. No therapeutic individualization is needed in the proposed indication based on demographic factors. As renal elimination of nirogacestat does not appear to be a primary clearance pathway, no dose adjustments are required in patients with renal impairment.
L451: (b) (4)
L452: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L453: (b) (4)
L454: FDA recommends that no dosage adjustment is recommended in patients with mild, moderate, or severe HI. See FDA's assessment in Section 6.3.2.3 below for details.
L455: ## 6.2.5. Outstanding Issues
L456: ## The Applicant's Position:
L457: As discussed during the Pre-NDA meeting, the Phase 1 study NIR-DT-104 evaluating the effects of hepatic impairment on the PK of nirogacestat is ongoing. An interim PK analysis was submitted in the NDA.
L458: As described in Section 6.2.1 and Section 10, the pediatric study, ARST1921, is currently ongoing. As stated in the Agreed iPSp letter, SpringWorks has requested a deferral of pediatric studies in the NDA.
L459: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L460: The Applicant submitted an interim PPK report, which was sufficient to evaluate the effect of hepatic impairment on nirogacestat PK; thus, hepatic impairment is no longer an outstanding issue.
L461: The review team will issue a PMC to evaluate the effects of a proton pump inhibitor (PPI) on the PK of nirogacestat.
L462: ## 6.3. Comprehensive Clinical Pharmacology Review
L463: ## 6.3.1. General Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetic Characteristics
L464: ## Data:
L465: Drug-drug interaction (DDI) risks are summarized in Section 6.2.4. Clinical pharmacology and an overview of ADME properties are provided below.
L466: **Clinical Pharmacology:** Study A8641001 demonstrated that, following single oral doses of nirogacestat in healthy participants, mean exposures as measured by AUC and C_{max} increased proportionally up to the 5-mg dose group, with greater than dose proportional increases observed from 10 to 40 mg. A less than proportional increase in AUC and C_{max} was noted between the 40 mg and 65 mg doses and a slightly greater than dose proportional increase was observed during the final dose escalation from 65 mg to 120 mg. In Study A8641002, nirogacestat exposures (AUC and C_{max}) in healthy participants increased with increasing dose over the 7 mg BID to 95 mg QD dose range following repeat administration for 14 days. Nirogacestat systemic exposures were slightly greater than dose proportional across all dose groups. In Study A8641014, nirogacestat exhibited generally dose proportional behavior (AUC_{tau} and C_{max}) over the dose range tested in participants with advanced solid tumor. In summary, nirogacestat has a tendency toward slightly greater than dose proportional increases in exposure below 100 mg and somewhat dose proportional increases between 100 mg and 220 mg.
L467: PK parameters tend to have substantial inter-subject variability as most coefficient of variation (CV) values were &gt; 30% and even approached 100% in some cases. The geometric mean for C_{max} was 612 ng/mL in Part 3 of Study NIR-DT-103 and 534 ng/mL in Part 2 of Study NIR-DT-102 but ranged from 822 to 943 ng/mL in the other studies. Similar patterns are observed with these studies for other parameters as well, with AUC_{inf} being lower and apparent clearance (CL/F) higher than in other studies. While these deviations from the other studies are notable, the geometric mean values from these study parts are within the range of variability observed in all studies. The median T_{max} was approximately 1 hour across studies. In general, the central tendency and large inter-subject variability for single-dose PK parameters for nirogacestat at 150 mg have been consistent across studies. Rich PK sampling in multiple dose studies using doses at or greater than 100 mg BID has only been conducted in Study A8641014. Therefore, the generalized values for C_{max} (1246 ng/mL) and AUC_{tau} (6430 ng\cdoth/mL) at steady-state are taken from the 150 mg BID evaluation at C1D21 in Study A8641014.
L468: In Study A8641002, the median accumulation ratio at Day 14 for healthy participants was 1.12, 1.35, and 1.73 for regimens of 95 mg QD, 40 mg QD, and 7 mg BID, respectively. In Study A8641014, the median accumulation ratio at Day 21 for participants with advanced solid tumors ranged from 1.18 to 1.6 between 20 mg BID and 80 mg BID and ranged from 1.98 to 2.84 between 100 mg BID and 220 mg BID.
L469: **Absorption:** Nirogacestat is rapidly absorbed, with median T_{max} observed approximately 1 hour following administration across all studies. Comparison of exposures from an intravenous dose of radiolabeled nirogacestat and orally administered nirogacestat in Study NIR-DT-102 determined a mean absolute bioavailability of 19.2% (range: 16.2% to 24.3). Based on the high
L470: level of radiolabel recovery in the mass balance assessments from this study and the increase in bioavailability observed when concomitantly administered with itraconazole in Study NIR-DT-103, it is likely that the absolute bioavailability of nirogacestat is limited by a strong first pass metabolism effect in the gastrointestinal tract.
L471: **Distribution:** Data suggest that the distribution of nirogacestat may be limited by serum AAG levels and that nirogacestat may be more widely distributed when AAG is low. The potential for wide distribution is also supported by the results of the PopPK analysis. Observations of low exposure in the central nervous system (CNS) may be due to low bioavailability of free nirogacestat and insufficient distribution throughout the body after a single dose.
L472: **Metabolism:** Nirogacestat is primarily metabolized by CYP3A4. Nirogacestat is extensively metabolized and leaves no major structural metabolites that would be active against GS. No major circulating active metabolites are believed to be active against GS.
L473: **Excretion:** Nirogacestat is extensively metabolized in the gastrointestinal tract and by hepatic metabolism. Feces is the primary route of excretion for drug-related material. While renal excretion is an insignificant route of elimination for unchanged nirogacestat, renal clearance may be an important route of elimination for some metabolites of nirogacestat.
L474: ## The Applicant's Position:
L475: Nirogacestat is rapidly absorbed with the potential for wide distribution. Nirogacestat is extensively metabolized, and the primary route of excretion is through feces, with very little unchanged nirogacestat recovered in feces or urine. No major circulating metabolites were identified.
L476: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L477: FDA generally agrees with the Applicant's assessment.
L478: FDA emphasizes that the reported PK parameters may not be directly applicable to patients with DT. The population PK model reported that clearance was more than two-fold higher in patients with DT (9.1 L/hr) compared to that of patients with other diseases (solid tumor, T-cell acute lymphoblastic leukemia/lymphoblastic lymphoma, 4.4L/hr).
L479: The review team does not agree with PK parameters based on all PK samples in patients with DT because certain PK samples were analyzed outside of the established long-term stability (LTS) window of 520 days (refer to OSIS review below). Therefore, these PK samples from 4 patients - (subject ID: [REDACTED](b)(6)) were excluded. The updated PK parameters reflecting patients with DT and excluding samples outside of the stability window are shown in Table 16. Section 12 in the nirogacestat USPI reflects PK data
L480: from patients with DT excluding the patients whose samples were analyzed outside of the LTS window.

L483: (Data Source: Table 1 on page 2 in the Applicant's response to FDA IR submitted on 8/2/2023)
L484: FDA agrees with the Applicant to report the nirogacestat excretion results in Part 1 of Study NIR-DT-102 in the labeling given the excretion pathway is not affected by the dosage forms and formulation (see drug formulation information in Table 17). Following oral administration of 5 µCi of [14C]-nirogacestat, the mean cumulative recovery in urine and feces combined was 55%, with 17% being excreted in urine and 38% in feces. Only a small fraction (0.07%) of the dose was recovered unchanged in urine. The results of recovery in feces and urine were similar to what was observed in Study NIR-DT-101 in which 44% and 18% was recovered in the feces and urine, respectively. In addition, radioactivity collected in expired air trapping solutions contributed a further 9.7% for a total cumulative recovery of 65% over the course of 312 hours post-dose, while in Part 2 of Study NIR-DT-102, radioactivity collected in expired air trapping
L485: solutions contributed a further 25% for a total cumulative recovery of 79% following an IV administration of nirogacestat. These results indicated that ^{14}\text{C}-labeled nirogacestat was released as a result of extensive metabolism and was ultimately eliminated as ^{14}\text{CO}_2. Of note, Studies NIR-DT-101 and NIR-DT-102 were both designed as mass balance studies following a single oral dose of [^{14}\text{C}]-nirogacestat, however, Study NIR-DR-102 also included radioactivity data collected in expired air and PK data following IV administration of [^{14}\text{C}]-nirogacestat. Therefore, Study NIR-DR-102 was chosen to report the data in full expansion of nirogacestat excretion and absolute bioavailability.
L486: ## Drug Formulation
L487: Multiple drug formulations were used during clinical development, including powder in bottle (PIB), citrate buffer solution (CBS), and tablets (uncoated and film-coated). The film-coated tablets are the proposed to-be-market formulation, which were only used in the open-label extension (OLE) phase in Study NIR-DT-301. Table 17 lists drug formulations in each trial.

L492: Although the Applicant did not conduct a relative bioavailability study between PiB, CBS and tablets, sufficient data were submitted to support using all of the data collected for these formulations in PK evaluation. PK data from the studies in which PiB and CBS were administered were useful to inform the population PK modeling and describe the absorption information at the lower end of the dose range.

Therefore, the absolute BA of nirogacestat (19%) in Part 2 of Study NIR-DT-102 following oral administration of 150 mg in uncoated tablets is included in the labeling. Generally, PK parameters of the film-coated tablets should be that of the PK parameters estimated for the uncoated tablets.
L494: ## 6.3.2. Clinical Pharmacology Questions
L495: ## 6.3.3. Does the clinical pharmacology program provide supportive evidence of effectiveness?
L496: ## The Applicant's Position:
L497: Yes. The effectiveness of nirogacestat 150 mg BID in participants with DT was demonstrated by the statistically significant improvement in progression-free survival (PFS) (HR = 0.22; 95% CI: 0.11, 0.42; one-sided p &lt; 0.001) and response rates of 31% in Study 14-C-0007 and 41% vs 8% for nirogacestat vs placebo in Study NIR-DT-301 and the relative tolerability.
L498: Exposure-response relationships were explored for several efficacy and safety endpoints using data from Studies A8641014 and NIR-DT-301, and the final model from the PopPK analysis. An exposure-response plateau was achieved within the predicted exposure ranges (AUCtau and
L499: C_{min}) for the \ge 100 mg to 150 mg dose range. Reduction in tumor size was independent of nirogacestat exposure within the evaluated dose range, whether it was determined by volumetric MRI (NIR-DT-301 only) or using RECIST (by CT or MRI). Similarly, there was a treatment effect relationship with PFS that was independent of exposure within the evaluated dose ranges. Additional analyses were conducted to explore if a relationship between nirogacestat exposure and objective response could be described; however, exposure was not found to be a significant predictor of duration of response due to low number of participants with loss of response.
L500: The exposure-response data support a recommended starting dose of 150 mg for all patients with allowance for dose modification based on tolerability to ensure optimal exposure for each individual.
L501: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L502: Yes, FDA agrees that the clinical pharmacology program provides supportive evidence of effectiveness by exposure-response (E-R) analyses for efficacy and tumor size.
L503: E-R analyses were performed between nirogacestat exposure and progression-free survival (PFS) based on clinical data in Study A8641014 and Study NIR-DT-301. The PFS probability for all tumors was not associated with nirogacestat exposures at the evaluated dose range. Higher exposure appeared to correlate with a longer response, though no exposure response relationship was observed for the duration of response which could be due to small exposure range for a single dose level. Nirogacestat-mediated treatment effect on tumor size was independent of nirogacestat exposure at the evaluated dose range in participants with DT and non-DT solid tumors. Predicted change in tumor size following treatment with nirogacestat at doses \ge 100 mg BID after 24 months was -22% (RECIST sum of longest diameter) and -55% (tumor volume), respectively. In addition, the PK/PD model for tumor size summarized by real time dose in Study NIR-DT-301 indicated comparable slope of tumor size decrease between patients with and without dose reduction. Refer to the pharmacometrics review in appendix 19.4.3 for details.
L504: ## 6.3.4. Is the proposed dosing regimen appropriate for the general patient population for which the indication is being sought?
L505: ## The Applicant's Position:
L506: Yes. The proposed dose of 150 mg BID is effective for patients with DT, with a positive benefit:risk. Subgroup analyses did not reveal the need for different doses based on sex, race, age, or weight. In the PopPK analyses, no demographic categories were selected as covariates
L507: that may alter PK. Most AEs were effectively managed with dose interruptions until recovery, dose reduction to 100 mg BID, and/or supportive care.
L508: Exposure-response relationships were explored for several efficacy and safety endpoints using data from Studies A8641014 and NIR-DT-301, and the final model from the Population PK Analysis. Exposure-response relationships were explored between nirogacestat exposure and Grade \ge 3 diarrhea, Grade \ge 3 rash, treatment-related TEAEs, SAEs, and TEAEs leading to discontinuation. No significant relationships were found with these endpoints.
L509: A statistically significant relationship was found between nirogacestat exposure and Grade \ge 3 hypophosphatemia. Compared to treatment, C_{max} was found to be the best predictor of Grade \ge 3 hypophosphatemia. Based on PopPK predicted median steady-state C_{max} values for participants with DT, this exposure-response model predicts a 6.3% probability of experiencing a Grade \ge 3 hypophosphatemia event at the 100 mg dose and a 7.2% probability at the 150 mg dose. This implies that the 150 mg dose carries a 0.9% absolute increased risk of Grade \ge 3 hypophosphatemia compared to the 100 mg dose.
L510: Additional analyses were performed for ovarian dysfunction (OD) and follicle-stimulating hormone (FSH). No exposure-response relationship could be identified with OD at the evaluated dose ranges, but a linear relationship between nirogacestat concentration and FSH levels in women of childbearing potential (WOCBP) was established. FSH increased linearly with increasing nirogacestat serum concentrations; however, high variability in the drug effect, presumably due to menstrual cycle oscillation and sparse sampling, made precise estimation of drug effects difficult.
L511: A statistically significant relationship was found between exposure and dose modifications. Participants with higher predicted exposures (AUC_{\tau} and C_{min}) at steady-state were more likely to have had dose modifications than those with lower steady-state exposures. Given that the majority of dose modifications were due to TEAEs, this relationship may suggest that participants with lower nirogacestat clearance were more likely to experience TEAEs that required a dose modification.
L512: The exposure-response data support a recommended starting dose of 150 mg for all patients with allowance for dose modification based on tolerability to ensure optimal exposure for each individual.
L513: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L514: Clinical pharmacology data support the proposed recommended dosage of 150 mg BID with reduction to 100 mg BID for selected adverse reactions based on the following evidence.
L515: - A statistically significant improvement in the primary endpoint of PFS was observed for nirogacestat 150 mg BID compared placebo, with a 71% reduction in the risk of disease progression (HR = 0.29 [95% CI: 0.15, 0.55]; p &lt; 0.001) in the pivotal clinical study NIR-DT-301 (*refer to clinical review section 8 for details*).
- ORR, as one of the secondary endpoints, was statistically significantly higher (p &lt; 0.001) in the nirogacestat arm (41%) versus the placebo arm (8%) in Study NIR-DT-301.
- PRO assessments evaluated as part of the secondary endpoints at the prespecified timepoint of Cycle 10 generally favored the nirogacestat arm.
- The overall safety profile of nirogacestat is considered acceptable and clinically manageable. Most frequently (&gt; 30%) reported TEAEs in Study NIR-DT-301 and all patients with DT were diarrhea, nausea, fatigue, hypophosphatemia event, and rash maculopapular, which are expected with GS inhibition.
- Despite high rate of dose interruption and reduction (65%) in the nirogacestat arm, the relative dose intensity is high (87%) indicating the duration of dosage interruption was likely short.
- E-R analysis showed that BOR rate and DoR were similar between patients with DT with dose reductions and those w/o dose reduction.
- PK/PD model for tumor size summarized by real time dose in Study NIR-DT-301 indicated comparable tumor shrinkage between patients with and without dose reduction.
- Ovarian dysfunction (OD) is a known risk for GS inhibitors based on animal study and is one of the adverse events of special interest. The Applicant reported OD in 27 of the 36 women of childbearing potential (WOCBP, 75%) in the nirogacestat arm and none in the 37 WOCBP in the placebo arm in Study NIR-DT-301. However, there is uncertainty regarding the characterization of ovarian toxicity, including reversibility. The clinical team issued a PMR of ovarian toxicity to better characterize this toxicity and clinical outcome (*refer to clinical review section 8 for more details*).
- PK/PD analyses based on nonclinical and clinical data indicated that 100 mg BID may have a similar effect in tumor reduction as 150 mg BID, but the sample size for patients who received 100 mg BID is limited.
- E-R analyses showed positive relationships between nirogacestat exposure and decreases in serum phosphate, Grade 3+ TEAE and dosage modification as well as a positive trend between nirogacestat exposure and FSH/ovary dysfunction; however, the dose range used in the E-R analysis is narrow and the sample size of patients who
L516: received a dosage &lt; 150 mg BID is limited (refer to *pharmacometrics review in appendix 19.4.3 for details.*)
L517: The proposed recommended dosage of 150 mg BID was chosen as the RP2D based on the comparison in the safety profile between dose cohorts of 150 mg and 200 mg BID and the observed clinical response in 7 patients with DT in Study A8641014. Given insufficient data are available at nirogacestat at dose levels &lt;150 mg and no integrated approach inclusive of PK, PD, dose-response or exposure-response relationship, it is not known if a lower dose could provide clinically meaningful efficacy with less toxicity.
L518: Collectively, the proposed recommended dosage of 150 mg BID demonstrated a clinical meaningful efficacy in patients DT compared to that of placebo. Dosage modifications for selected AEs are included in the approved labeling to manage adverse reactions.
L519: ## 6.3.5. Is an alternative dosing regimen or management strategy required for subpopulations based on intrinsic patient factors (e.g. race, ethnicity, age, performance status, genetic subpopulations, etc.)?
L520: ## The Applicant's Position:
L521: No alternative dosing regimen or management strategy is required for subpopulations based on intrinsic patient factors in the PopPK analyses.
L522: (b) (4)
L523: As described in Section 6.2.4,
L524: **Renal Impairment:** Renal elimination of nirogacestat does not appear to be a primary clearance pathway as supported by the PK characteristics of nirogacestat. No dose adjustments are required for renal impairment.
L525: **Age:** No age-specific differences in TEAEs among adults were observed that would require a dosing management strategy. Nirogacestat PK has not been evaluated in pediatric populations to date. A clinical study of nirogacestat in pediatric participants with DT aged &gt; 12 months to &lt; 18 years of age is ongoing in collaboration with the COG (Study ARST1921). It is generally assumed that the mechanistic basis for nirogacestat in DT is the same between adults and children. Age was evaluated as a covariate in the PopPK analysis, which included 25 participants aged 65 years or older. No significant effect of age on PK was identified.
L526: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L527: FDA generally agrees with the Applicant's position that no alternative dosing regimen is required for subpopulations regarding age, sex, race and renal function based on the updated population PK analysis (refer to the *pharmacometrics review in appendix 19.4.2 for details*).
L528: ## Hepatic Impairment
L529: (b) (4)
L530: Instead, FDA recommends that no dosage adjustment is recommended for patients with mild, moderate, or severe HI.
L531: Study NIR-DT-104 evaluated nirogacestat PK in patients with impaired hepatic function. Severity of HI was determined using both the Child-Pugh Classification system and the NCI-ODWG criteria for liver dysfunction. Patients with Child-Pugh Class A (mild) HI or NCI-ODWG Group D (severe) HI were not included in the PK analysis. In total, 11 patients had Child-Pugh Class B (moderate) HI and 1 patient Child-Pugh Class C (severe) HI. Of note, 5, 2, and 4 out of 11 patients in the group of Child-Pugh Class B (moderate HI) were classified as NCI-ODWG Group A (normal hepatic function), Group B (mild HI) and Group C (moderate HI), respectively. The one patient in the group of Child-Pugh Class C (severe HI) was classified as NCI-ODWG Group C (moderate HI). Table 18 lists the geometric mean ratio [GMR (90% CI)] of nirogacestat exposure following a single dose of 100 mg in patients with moderate HI by either Child-Pugh Classification or NCI-ODWG criteria compared to that of 12 subjects with normal hepatic function. Overall, Cmax is lower in patients with moderate HI by either Child-Pugh Classification or NCI-ODWG criteria comparing to healthy subjects.

L536: Safety data from Study NIR-DT-104 were also assessed. Only 1 TEAE was reported in a patient, who had moderate HI by Child-Pugh and mild HI by NCI-ODWG criteria. This adverse reaction was assessed by the investigator as Grade 1 in severity, and the patient recovered the same day with no intervention. No Grade 3 or 4 adverse reactions were reported. In addition, no clinically significant shifts in clinical laboratory values or vital signs were noted following administration of nirogacestat. Although data are limited to a single dose, moderate hepatic impairment did not appear to significantly increase toxicity.
L537: In addition, per the communication with clinical team, the liver toxicity (AST or ALT elevation) caused by nirogacestat was considered mild as most AEs were low grade and can be reversed by dosage modifications.
L538: Despite limited clinical data in patients with severe HI (only one patient per NCI-ODWG criteria), the review team does not recommend a dosage adjustment in patients with severe HI. A large increase in nirogacestat exposure is not expected in patients with severe HI because there was no significant change in patients with mild or moderate HI. The proposed dosage modifications (e.g., down to 100 mg BID) are expected to be sufficient to manage any emerging toxicity. A PMR for a severe HI study was not issued due to feasibility concerns and because severe HI is not expected to be common in the indicated patient population.
L539: Therefore, combining the clinical AE evaluation on liver toxicity and the results of Study NIR-DT-104, dosage adjustments do not appear to be necessary for patients with mild or moderate HI classified using either Child-Pugh or NCI-ODWG criteria and a contraindication for HI is not justified given the lack of data in patients with severe HI.
L540: ## CTNNB1 mutations and treatment efficacy
L541: In study NIR-DT-301, genetic mutations (germline and somatic) in the *CTNNB1* and *APC* genes were centrally evaluated using blood sample and tumor biopsy collected at baseline. T41A (34.3%) and S45F (18.6%) were the most frequently observed mutations. Similar frequencies for mutations were observed in treatment arms and placebo arms. Among the 105 participants with evaluable tumor samples (of 142 enrolled), 85 participants (81%) had a *CTNNB1* mutation
L542: and 22 participants (21%) had an *APC* mutation. FDA explored the nirogacestat efficacy in subgroups with different *CTNNB1* mutations. Responses to nirogacestat treatment were observed across *CTNNB1* mutations (Table 19). The point estimate for the PFS HR was slightly lower in the subgroup of patients whose tumors had the S45F mutation (HR=0.18, 95% CI: 0.02-1.46, ORR=62%), compared to patient with T41A mutations (HR= 0.38, 95% CI: 0.13-1.10, ORR = 33%). The results suggested nirogacestat treatment is effective across *CTNNB1* mutations. The lack of differences in clinical response is consistent with the mechanism of action. However, there were absent of *in vitro*/animal pharmacology studies on different mutations in *CTNNB1* genes. See section 8.1.3.2 for additional clinical efficacy results stratified by mutation status.

L546: ## 6.3.6. Are there clinically relevant food-drug or drug-drug interactions, and what is the appropriate management strategy?
L547: ## The Applicant's Position:
L548: Due to strong dependency on CYP3A4 for elimination of nirogacestat, concomitant administration with strong or moderate inhibitors of CYP3A4 should be avoided. If a strong or
L549: moderate inhibitor of CYP3A4 must be prescribed, a patient should cease administration of nirogacestat for the duration of use of the interacting drug. (b) (4)
L550: There is no expectation of clinical benefit from nirogacestat if taken concomitantly with strong CYP3A4 inducers. Exposure to nirogacestat is predicted to be greatly reduced when administered concomitantly with moderate CYP3A4 inducers, but it is unknown if therapeutic benefit from nirogacestat could be expected. The effects of P-gp inhibition on nirogacestat were not evaluated in a clinical study, however any clinical effect is believed to be less important than the effect of first-pass metabolism by CYP3A4.
L551: Nirogacestat is a weak inhibitor of CYP3A4 with clinical usage. Concomitant usage of nirogacestat with CYP3A4 substrates that have a narrow therapeutic window should be avoided as nirogacestat may increase the exposure to a CYP3A4 substrate. Nirogacestat may cause weak-to-moderate induction of CYP2C19 in some cases. In a clinical evaluation with dabigatran etexilate, nirogacestat showed no meaningful effect on P-gp inhibition.
L552: The solubility of nirogacestat is substantially reduced at pH greater than 6.0, thus gastric acid reducing agents may reduce the bioavailability of nirogacestat. (b) (4)
L553: In an evaluation of food effects on nirogacestat PK in Study A8641014, C_{max} and AUC_{tau} were similar between fed and fasted states but carried significant variability. In Study NIR-DT-301, nirogacestat was dosed without regard to food. Given the observed efficacy and safety parameters from Study NIR-DT-301, nirogacestat is recommended for administration without regard to food.
L554: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L555: FDA agrees with the Applicant's position regarding the labeling recommendation for the concomitant use of nirogacestat with strong or moderate CYP3A inhibitors and inducers and drugs that are substrates of CYP3A4 and CYP2C19. However, FDA does not agree with the Applicant's position regarding the labeling recommendation for the concomitant use of nirogacestat with gastric acid-reducing agents (ARAs). FDA's rationale and supportive data are summarized below.
L556: ## Effect of Concomitant Medications on Nirogacestat
L557: a. Strong and moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors
L558: The effects of a strong CYP3A4 strong inhibitor increased AUC_{last}, AUC_{inf} and C_{max} of a single dose 100 mg of nirogacestat by approximately 6-, 8- and 2.5-fold, respectively.
L559: The Applicant developed a PBPK model to predict the potential DDI of nirogacestat under various scenarios following a single and multiple doses (100 mg or 150 mg) when co-administered with inhibitors of CYP3A4 in healthy subjects and patients. These simulations included the effect of coadministration of strong inhibitors (itraconazole, ketoconazole and clarithromycin), moderate inhibitors (fluconazole and erythromycin), and a weak inhibitor (cimetidine). Nirogacestat AUC is predicted to increase by 3.5 to 6.3 fold following coadministration of multiple doses of nirogacestat (150 mg BID) with strong inhibitors and increase by 2.7 to 3.2 folds with moderate CYP3A inhibitors (*Refer to the PBPK review in appendix 19.4.4 for details*).
L560: Considering the magnitude of the increase in AUCs of nirogacestat when co-administered with strong and moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors and the positive relationship between nirogacestat exposure and adverse reactions in the E-R analysis, FDA generally agrees with the Applicant's position that concomitant use with strong or moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors should be avoided. However, FDA does not agree with the proposed labeling language; (b) (4)
L561: FDA revised the labeling to avoid co-administration of strong or moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors or inducers with nirogacestat.
L562: ## b. Strong and moderate CYP3A4 inducers
L563: A PBPK model was used to predict the potential DDI between nirogacestat and a strong inducer (rifampicin) or a moderate inducer (efavirenz) following a single dose and multiple doses (100 mg or 150 mg BID) in healthy subjects and patients. The AUC of nirogacestat was predicted to decrease by 85% when co-administered with rifampicin and by 67% when co-administered with efavirenz with multiple doses of nirogacestat at 150 mg BID (*Refer to PBPK review in appendix 19.4.4 for details*).
L564: FDA generally agrees with the Applicant's position to avoid the coadministration of strong or moderate CYP3A4 inducers with nirogacestat. However, FDA does not agree with the proposed labeling language; (b) (4)
L565: FDA revised the labeling to avoid concomitant use of nirogacestat with strong or moderate CYP3A4 inducers.
L566: ## c. P-gp inhibitors
L567: The effect of concomitant use of itraconazole (a strong CYP3A4 inhibitor and a P-gp inhibitor) on nirogacestat PK was evaluated. The mean C_{max} of a single dose 100 mg of nirogacestat following multiple doses of itraconazole days was increased by approximately 2.5-fold and median T_{max} was delayed from 1 to 1.5 hours. The mass balance study revealed the absence of unchanged parent drug in feces after oral administration of 150 mg nirogacestat. Additionally, the nirogacestat PK exhibited linearity across the dosage range of 1-120 mg (SD) and 20-220 mg (BID) following oral administration. These findings indicated that the observed DDI with itraconazole was most likely a result of nirogacestat mediated CYP3A4 inhibition rather than the inhibition of P-gp. FDA agrees with the applicant that a P-gp inhibitor is unlikely to have a clinically meaningful effect of nirogacestat exposure.
L568: ## d. Gastric pH-altering agents
L569: Nirogacestat demonstrates pH-dependent solubility. The Applicant did not conduct clinical studies to evaluate the effects of gastric acid-reducing agents (ARAs), but the Applicant evaluated the effect of ARAs in the population PK model; the effect was inconclusive due to the low number of patients on ARAs. Therefore, a clinical pharmacology PMC is issued to evaluate the impact of concomitant use of a proton pump inhibitor on nirogacestat PK. Proton pump inhibitors were selected to best describe the worst-case scenario as they are the ARA with the strongest effect on pH relative to antacids and H2 receptor antagonists.
L570: FDA does not agree with the Applicant's proposed recommendations. FDA recommends:
L571: - Avoiding concomitant use with proton pump inhibitors and H2 receptor antagonists.
- Staggering administration of antacids (e.g., administer nirogacestat 2 hours before or 2 hours after antacid use).
L572: ## Effect of Nirogacestat on Concomitant Medication
L573: ## a. Substrates of CYP3A4
L574: Co-administration of nirogacestat with oral midazolam, a sensitive probe CYP3A substrate, increased midazolam plasma AUC by 59%, indicating that a nirogacestat is a weak CYP3A inhibitor.
L575: The PBPK model was used to predict the magnitude of interaction between nirogacestat and midazolam following multiple doses of nirogacestat of 150 mg BID. The C_{max} and AUC of midazolam was predicted to increase by 1.8 and 2.1 folds, respectively, suggesting that nirogacestat is a weak inhibitor of CYP3A4 at the proposed recommended dosage.
L576: Taken together, FDA agrees with the Applicant's labeling proposal that concomitant use with certain CYP3A4 substrates should be avoided where minimal concentration changes may lead to serious adverse reactions.
L577: ## **b. Substrates of CYP2B6, 2C8, 2C9 and 2C19**
L578: The Applicant did not conduct any clinical studies to evaluate the effects of nirogacestat-mediated induction of CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, and CYP2C19. No meaningful induction was predicted for nirogacestat on substrates of CYP2B6, CYP2C8, or CYP2C9 based on in vitro data and PBPK simulation. A moderate interaction was predicted for S-mephenytoin (a sensitive CYP2C19 substrate) when co-administering with nirogacestat at 100 mg and 150 mg BID using a 10-fold increase in maximum fold induction to assess a worst-case scenario, although the in vitro study indicated that the risk of DDI between nirogacestat and CYP2C19 substrates is low. Therefore, the potential induction of nirogacestat on certain CYP2C19 substrates cannot be ruled out.
L579: Taken together, FDA agrees with the Applicant's labeling proposal that concomitant use with certain CYP2C19 substrates should be avoided where minimal concentration changes may lead to significant decreases in efficacy.
L580: ## **c. Substrates of P-gp**
L581: The results of Study NIR-DT-103 showed that nirogacestat is unlikely to inhibit P-gp. The C_{max} and AUC for total dabigatran was similar when administered alone (C_{max} = 76 ng/mL and AUC_{inf} = 600 h*ng/mL) or with a single dose of nirogacestat at 150 mg (C_{max} = 73 ng/mL and AUC_{inf} = 587 h*ng/mL). Labeling states that exposure to dabigatran (P-gp substrate) C_{max} and AUC were not affected by concomitant use with nirogacestat.
L582: ## **Food Effect**
L583: FDA agrees with the Applicant's position that a high fat and high calorie meal had no clinical meaningful effect on nirogacestat PK based on the results from the food-effect sub-study in the EXPAND-S phase of Study A8641014. Briefly, dose normalized mean C_{max} (n=13) and AUC_{tau} (n=11) were similar in the fed (5.2 ng/mL and 22.5 ng*hr/mL, respectively) and fasted condition (6.6 ng/mL and 23.7 ng*hr/mL, respectively) following 150 mg and 220 mg BID (Table 20). Considering the magnitude of the change in nirogacestat exposure and the range of 90%
L584: confidence interval (CI), the overall changes in AUC_{\tau} and C_{max} in the fed condition are not expected to be clinically meaningful.
L585: **Table 20: Geometric Least Squares Mean Ratio (GMR) of Nirogacestat Exposure in Food Effect Group**
