OSIS Reviewer's Assessment
The method showed the acceptable performance for nirogacestat in the presence of 32,400 ng/mL rifampicin, 2,360 ng/mL digoxin, 300 ng/mL dabigatran etexilate (V2007078.02 Addendum 1), or 4000 ng/mL itraconazole (V2007078.02 Addendum 2). Hemolysis and lipemia had no notable effect on assay performance. Seven cycles of freeze-thaw stability were established and acceptable. For assessments not evaluated in method validation V2007078.02 (i.e., stock solution stability, long-term stability), references were made to the nirogacestat method validation report A8649007 conducted by (b) (4), which is considered acceptable.
It was noted that in clinical study NIR-DT-301, there were 604 human serum samples initially analyzed by (b) (4) then reanalyzed by (b) (4). The explanation for reanalysis and the dataset of the 604 samples initially analyzed by (b) (4) were requested in the Information Request (conveyed on 04/05/2023). The Applicant provided response and dataset on 04/12/2023. This Reviewer calculated the mean difference of the drug concentration values between the initially analyzed from (b) (4) and the reanalyzed from (b) (4), resulting in 3.5\% \pm 10.9\% SD by using the equation for incurred sample reanalysis (ISR): (repeat – original)/average * 100. The Information Request response appears reasonable per the OSIS perspective, but OSIS finds the results of the 32 samples analyzed outside of the 520-day long-term stability unreliable (please see Information Request and Response section below for details).
Overall, partial method validation V2007078.02 and related amendments appear to be appropriate for quantifying nirogacestat in human serum for bioanalytical studies (b) (4) 2007079.01, (b) (4) 009104.01, and (b) (4) 101086.02 supporting clinical studies NIR-DT-301, NIR-DT-103, and NIR-DT-104.
L1935: ## Information Request and Response
L1936: (b) (4)
L1937: ## **OSIS Recommendation**
L1938: Due to the inability of [REDACTED] (b) (4) to extend the long-term stability from the 520 days established by [REDACTED] (b) (4) the results of the 32 samples assayed by [REDACTED] (b) (4) outside of the 520-day stability window are unreliable. This concern has been communicated to OCP.

L1954: Due to the limited data information included in method validation report 193/006V, the quantification of ^{14}\text{C}-nirogacestat in human serum is considered not fully validated from the OSIS perspective, particularly with diluted samples (e.g., those listed in section 11.5 of report 193/007). For the percentage of ^{14}\text{C} radioactivity in excretion reported in other matrices (i.e., urine, feces, and expired air), there was no method validation for OSIS to review. OSIS has communicated these issues to OCP review team and will defer OCP's final determination of the usage of the data to support labeling (OCP's assessment is described in the review above).
L1955: ## 19.4.4. Population PK Analysis
L1956: ## 19.4.5. Executive Summary
L1957: A series of clinical studies (including DDI and ADME studies) have been completed to characterize the clinical pharmacology of nirogacestat. Nirogacestat was rapidly absorbed and T_{max} was reached between 0.5-2 hours across all evaluated doses. Nirogacestat concentration-time profiles showed multi-phasic disposition. Following repeat doses of nirogacestat, steady-state appears to have been achieved by approximately Day 7. The absolute bioavailability (F) of nirogacestat following oral administration of a 150 mg dose was 19.2%.
L1958: Nirogacestat is a sensitive substrate of CYP3A4 and subject to DDI based on CYP3A4 metabolism. Following administration of 100 mg nirogacestat with itraconazole, a strong CYP3A4 inhibitor and clinical inhibitor of P-gp, nirogacestat C_{max} and AUC_{inf} were increased by 2.5- and 8.2-fold, respectively, compared to when given alone. Renal excretion of nirogacestat is negligible (0.020 to 0.042% recovered unchanged in urine).
L1959: A PopPK model was developed to support the dose rationale for nirogacestat and is included in this NDA. This activity included the following objectives:
L1960: - Develop a PopPK model to characterize the PK of nirogacestat in healthy subjects and participants;
- Evaluate the effect of covariates on the variability of the PK of nirogacestat;
- Evaluate the magnitude and clinical relevance, when possible, of the statistically significant findings from the analysis;
- Predict exposures following doses of nirogacestat under scenarios of clinical importance, if applicable
L1961: **Methods:** Nonlinear mixed effects modeling was used to develop the PopPK model using data from healthy volunteers and oncology participants following single or multiple dose oral administration of nirogacestat. Model building started with a simple one compartment, first-order absorption model and increased step-wise in complexity until further improvement in fit was not supported by the data. The impact of structural covariates (e.g. dose and DDI), study-specific parameters and individual subject characteristics (e.g. body weight and participant population) were assessed using manual forward addition/backward elimination. The final model was qualified by numerical and graphical goodness of fit (GOF) checks, including visual predictive checks (VPCs).
L1962: **Results:** PK data from 335 healthy adult volunteers and oncology participants, collected in six healthy volunteer (A8641001, A8641002, A8641008, NIR-DT-101, NIR-DT-102, and NIR-DT-103) and two oncology participant studies (A8641014 and NIR-DT-301), were available for the PopPK analysis. The disposition and elimination of nirogacestat in serum was best described by a three-compartment model with lag-time, first-order absorption kinetics after oral administration.
L1963: Clearance (CL) was 9.09 L/h in healthy volunteers and participants with DT. In participants with solid tumors or T-ALL/LBL, CL was lower and estimated to be 4.4 L/h. Estimates for central volume of distribution (V_c), peripheral volume of distribution - shallow compartment (V_{p1}) and peripheral volume of distribution – deep compartment (V_{p2}) were 7.74 L, 11.5 L, and 115 L, respectively.
L1964: Following oral administration, nirogacestat was rapidly absorbed through a lag-time, first-order absorption process. Absorption in Study NIR-DT-301 was slower (absorption rate constant (k_a) = 0.38 h^{-1}) compared to the Phase 1 studies (k_a = 0.876 h^{-1}).
L1965: In addition to the participant-specific elimination, body weight, CYP3A4 inhibition, and dose were each found to have a statistically significant influence on the nirogacestat PK. V_{p2} was found to increase proportionally with weight. Nirogacestat was subject to CYP3A4-based DDI with approximately 4.7-fold lower CL (CL = 1.94 L/h) and F increased 2-fold (absolute F = 38%) following co-administration with the strong CYP3A4 inhibitor itraconazole. Other co-medications did not appear to affect nirogacestat PK, but inference may be inconclusive given the overall low number of subjects.
L1966: A trend between F and dose was seen at lower doses. F was found to be dose-dependent and asymptotically increased to a value of 19.2% as dose levels increased. The dose-dependent effect was not meaningful at doses equal or greater than 10 mg.
L1967: ## **Conclusions:**
L1968: - Nirogacestat PK was adequately characterized by a three-compartment, lag-time, first-order absorption model.
- Study-phase specific differences in k_a and residual variability were observed, with slower nirogacestat absorption, shorter Tlag, and a distinct error model for Study NIR-DT-301 – residual variability was also dependent on the bioanalytical lab used for serum quantification.
- Administration of nirogacestat with a powder-in-bottle formulation in early Phase 1 studies resulted in a shorter Tlag and lower F as compared to tablet formulations.
- CL was lower in participants with solid tumors or T-ALL/LBL (4.4 L/h) compared to healthy volunteers and participants with DT (9.09 L/h).
L1969: - V_{p2} was found to increase proportionally with weight.
- Strong CYP3A4 inhibitors affected nirogacestat PK, with approximately 4.7-fold lower CL and 2-fold higher F following itraconazole co-administration.
- F was dose-dependent and asymptotically increased with increasing doses. For doses equal or greater than 20 mg, F converged to a value of 19.2%.
L1970: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L1971: FDA agrees.
L1972: ## 19.4.6. PPK Assessment Summary
L1973: ## The Applicant's Position:
L1974: The disposition and elimination of nirogacestat in serum is best described by a three-compartment model with lag-time and first-order absorption kinetics after oral administration. Key clinically relevant covariate relationships described include an effect for CYP3A4 inhibition on CL and F, weight on the peripheral volume compartments, and participant population on CL. Notably, the PK of nirogacestat in participants with DT was more similar to that observed in healthy volunteers than to nirogacestat PK described in participants with advanced solid tumors (other than DT) and T-ALL/LBL (collectively referred to as advanced malignancies for the PopPK analyses). This difference was explained by a 52% reduction in the fixed effects estimate for CL in participants with advanced malignancies relative to healthy volunteers and participants with DT.

Uncertainty and Variability (RSE, IIV, Shrinkage, Bootstrap)	
In the final model, all parameters were estimated with sufficient precision with RSE < 50%. \eta-shrinkage was determined to be 11%, 57%, 15%, 18%, 28%, 42%, and 19% for the evaluated parameters of F, k_a, CL, V_c, V_{p2}, Tlag, and itraconazole-mediated inhibition of CYP3A4, respectively. IIV (%CV) for these parameters were 62% (F), 13% (k_a), 32.9% (CL), 93.7% (V_c), 30.1% (V_{p2}), 24.1% (Tlag), and 27.2% (itraconazole inhibition).
Residual variability was described by a proportional error model with estimates of 17.8% CV ((b) (4) bioanalytical lab), 47.1% CV (patient studies), 24.1% CV ((b) (4) and 13.3% CV ((b) (4) lab). Generally, residual variability was larger in the patient studies (A8641014 and NIR-DT-301) compared to the healthy volunteer studies.
Large inter- and intra-study variability was observed in serum exposures; however, the model was able to reasonably capture the central tendency and observed variability. The model tended to under predict serum concentrations around Tmax in Study NIR-DT-301. The under-prediction, however, may be the consequence of the complex, variable absorption of nirogacestat, which may not be entirely captured by the simple lag-time, first order absorption model, particularly with highly variable data from the Phase 3 trial.
Yes.
Despite that underprediction of Cmax was observed, individual profiles of patients with DT in Study NIR-DT-301 fit the sparse PK observed on Cycle 1 Day 1 and steady state reasonably well.
L1978: BLQ for Parameter Accuracy	The LLOQ for all methods was 0.5 ng/mL. BLQ values were assumed to be 0.5*LLOQ (0.25 ng/mL). Since post-dose BLQ frequency was relatively low and rather inflated by the low doses of Study A8641001 and the late sampling times in NIR-DT-101 and NIR-DT-102, it was deemed that missing values did not unduly influence model building. As such, likelihood-based approaches to handling BLQ were not considered.	Yes

[FDA's comments]
12.3 PK	General nirogacestat PK parameters are summarized in USPI Table 6. Nirogacestat is rapidly absorbed with bioavailability limited by significant first-pass metabolism. Exposure is similar between fed and fasted states, and nirogacestat was dosed without regard to food in Study NIR-DT-301. Nirogacestat is highly protein bound and widely distributed throughout the body. Nirogacestat is	Generally acceptable.
PK parameters in Table 6 of USPI were updated based on popPK analysis to provide values for patients with desmoid tumor in place of values for patients with T-ALL/LBL and solid tumors who had 52% lower CL.
L1979: extensively metabolized by CYP3A4, with metabolized drug product recovered in feces, urine, and expired air. Less than 0.1% of unchanged nirogacestat is recovered in urine.
Specific Populations
Effects of Age, Gender and Race
A PopPK analysis of nirogacestat was performed, which found no clinically meaningful effect of age, gender, or race on the PK of nirogacestat.
Effects of Hepatic Impairment
The PK of nirogacestat were evaluated in participants with moderate to severe hepatic impairment based on Child-Pugh classification and moderate hepatic impairment based on NCI-ODWG classification. Participants classified as moderate by Child-Pugh classification with total bilirubin levels 1.5 \times \text{ULN} or lower (i.e., those that did not meet the criteria for moderate impairment by NCI-ODWG) did not exhibit significant differences in nirogacestat PK. Some participants that met the criteria for moderate impairment by NCI-ODWG (total bilirubin > 1.5 \times \text{ULN}) exhibited lower C_{\text{max}}, higher volume of distribution, longer half-lives, and greater overall exposure compared to healthy controls. Therefore, nirogacestat is contraindicated in patients diagnosed with moderate or severe hepatic impairment per NCI-ODWG criteria (total bilirubin > 1.5 \times \text{ULN}).
Effects of Renal Impairment
The effects of renal impairment on nirogacestat PK have not been evaluated in a clinical trial; however, in a PopPK model no meaningful relationship was observed between renal function tests and nirogacestat PK. Clinical studies determined that nirogacestat is extensively metabolized, and no major metabolites were found in urine. Only small amounts of unchanged nirogacestat were detected in the urine.
The effect of hepatic impairment has only been assessed in dedicated study given no patients with hepatic impairment was included in PPK assessment.
Based on PPK assessment, CYP3A strong inhibitors (i.e., itraconazole) is expected to have clinically meaningful impact on exposure (9.3 folds higher) and is recommended against concomitant use.
L1980: Drug Interaction Studies
Effect of other drugs on nirogacestat
Nirogacestat is a sensitive CYP3A4 substrate. In vitro studies with human liver microsomes indicated that nirogacestat is primarily metabolized by the drug-metabolizing enzyme CYP3A4 (85%). The effect of CYP3A4 inhibitors on nirogacestat was evaluated with itraconazole, a strong CYP3A4 inhibitor, in healthy participants. Using clinical and in vitro data, a mechanistic PBPK model was developed to simulate interaction scenarios with various CYP3A4 inhibitors and inducers.
Co-administration of nirogacestat with strong and moderate inhibitors of CYP3A4 may increase serum nirogacestat concentrations exceeding those observed at the maximum tolerated dose.
Co-administration of nirogacestat with strong and moderate inducers of CYP3A4 may decrease serum nirogacestat concentration to poorly effective levels.
Co-administration of nirogacestat with strong CYP3A4 inducers may result in negligible nirogacestat exposure. Co-administration of nirogacestat with moderate CYP3A inducers may result in significant decrease of nirogacestat exposure, but not to a negligible level.
In vitro permeability studies and knockout mouse studies indicate that nirogacestat may be a substrate of the P-gp transporter. Since itraconazole may also inhibit P-gp, the results of the clinical evaluation with itraconazole may also be in part due to P-gp inhibition. CYP3A4 inhibition, however, is the dominant interaction.
The solubility of nirogacestat is substantially reduced at pH greater than 6.0. No clinical studies have been conducted to evaluate the effects of drugs that increase gastric pH, such as proton pump inhibitors or H2-receptor antagonists, on nirogacestat; however, co-administration of these drugs with
L1981: nirogacestat may reduce the bioavailability of nirogacestat.
Effect of nirogacestat on other drugs
The effects of nirogacestat on midazolam, a sensitive CYP3A4 substrate, were evaluated in clinical studies of healthy participants. Using clinical and in vitro data, a mechanistic PBPK model was developed to simulate interaction scenarios with substrates of CYP3A4, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, and CYP2C19. Nirogacestat is a weak inhibitor of CYP3A4 and administration of nirogacestat may increase the exposure of CYP3A substrates. No meaningful effects were predicted for nirogacestat on substrates of CYP2B6, CYP2C8, and CYP2C9.
PBPK simulations predicted minimal-to-weak induction of CYP2C19, however a sensitivity analysis under worst-case conditions predicted potential for moderate induction. Effects of nirogacestat on CYP2C19 substrates have not been evaluated in a clinical study; however, weak-to-moderate induction may be possible.

L2013: ## **19.4.9. Exposure-Response Analysis**
L2014: ## **19.4.10. ER (efficacy) Executive Summary**
L2015: PK/PDx models were developed to evaluate the PK/PDx and exposure-response relationship (ERR) between nirogacestat and efficacy endpoints in participants with DT, which included the following objectives:
L2016: - Characterize the PopPKPDx relationship of nirogacestat exposure and changes in tumor size;
- Characterize the PopPKPDx relationship of nirogacestat exposure and PFS;
- Characterize the ERR between nirogacestat exposure and BOR and DOR;
L2017: **Methods:** The previously developed nirogacestat PopPK model was used to derive individual exposure parameters. Nonlinear mixed effects modeling was employed to develop PKPDx models to characterize safety and efficacy of nirogacestat in participants with DT, participants with solid tumors other than DT, and participants with T-ALL/LBL. The binary outcome for PFS was analyzed using a time to event model, whereas tumor size was analyzed as a continuous variable. Model development was carried out sequentially, first describing disease progression and placebo response, followed by assessment of the nirogacestat ERR. Covariate-parameter relationships of interest were explored graphically and formally tested if substantial trends were evident. Final PKPDx models were qualified by VPCs, in addition to numerical checks of parameter precision and model robustness.
L2018: Bayesian posthoc PK parameter estimates were used to estimate nirogacestat exposures (nominal steady state or exposure at day of onset) for the subsequent exposure-response analysis. For each safety endpoint, nominal steady state exposure was used for participants with no event. To assess the impact of changes in exposure for DOR and BOR, exposure-
L2019: response relationships were graphically evaluated in all participants and in nirogacestat treated participants only. Significant relationships were further examined by comparing treatment effect models (i.e., to assess response for nirogacestat treated participants compared to placebo participants) to exposure-response models (i.e., to assess changes in response as nirogacestat concentration changes). DOR was evaluated using exploratory Kaplan-Meier plots and BOR, was evaluated by logistic regression.
L2020: **Results:** A total of 212 participants were available for PFS evaluation. Of those, 150 (70.8%) were participants with DT. The hazard for DT, as a measure of PFS, was 19-fold lower than for other tumors. PFS probability was associated with nirogacestat treatment, independent of nirogacestat exposures, at the evaluated dose range. The PFS HR estimate for treatment relative to no treatment was 0.26. Age and sex were not associated with PFS.
L2021: Changes in tumor size over time were characterized by RECIST (DT and other tumors) and tumor volume (DT only) models comprising a first-order tumor growth rate, which was flexible to describe both disease progression and regression in the tumor volume model, and a first-order tumor shrinkage rate. Nirogacestat-mediated tumor regression was described by an on-off treatment effect. An ERR between nirogacestat AUC and tumor sizes could not be established at the evaluated nirogacestat exposure ranges. Tumor size was predicted to change by -22% (RECIST sum of longest diameter) and -55% (tumor volume) following treatment with nirogacestat at doses \ge 100 mg BID for 24 months. Tumor type, APC tumor suppressor genotype and \beta-catenin genotype (CTNNB1) were not associated with change in tumor size.
L2022: Thirty-six nirogacestat-treated participants (17.3%) and 5 placebo participants (2.4%) were found to have a CR or PR. A statistically significant negative trend was found for nominal C_{max}, C_{min}, AUC at steady state only when placebo participants were excluded from evaluation, indicating that responses occur at lower exposures compared to non-responders across in the nirogacestat treated groups. However, the negative trend was driven by the lack of efficacy and higher exposures observed in patients with non-DT tumor types. The BOR relationship with nominal steady state nirogacestat exposure was not found to be significant in participants with DT alone. Higher exposure did appear to correlate with a longer response, though exposure was not a statistically significant predictor of duration of response.
L2023: **Conclusion:** This analysis represents a comprehensive examination of PKPDx data collected from a Phase 1 trial of nirogacestat in participants with advanced solid tumors and participants with relapsed or refractory T-ALL/LBL, and a Phase 3 trial of nirogacestat in participants with DT. Principal findings of the efficacy analyses are described below.
L2024: ## **Efficacy:**
L2025: - For PFS, the HR estimate for solid tumors and T-ALL/LBL relative to DT tumors was 19. The PFS probability for all tumors was not associated with nirogacestat exposures at the
L2026: evaluated dose range but was described by a nirogacestat treatment effect. The HR estimate for treatment relative to no treatment was 0.26.
L2027: - There was a nirogacestat-mediated treatment effect on tumor size, which was independent of nirogacestat exposure at the evaluated dose range in participants with DT and non-DT solid tumors.
- Predicted change in tumor size following treatment with nirogacestat at doses \ge 100 mg BID after 24 months was -22% (RECIST sum of longest diameter) and -55% (tumor volume), respectively.
- Thirty-six nirogacestat-treated participants (17.3%) and 5 participants receiving placebo (2.4%) were found to have a CR or PR. A negative trend for nominal steady state exposure was observed for nirogacestat-treated participants, indicating that responses may occur at lower exposures compared to non-responders. This assessment, however, is confounded by the larger exposures and lack of efficacy in participants with non-DT tumors. The BOR relationship with nominal steady state nirogacestat exposure was not found to be significant in participants with DT alone.
- Higher exposure appeared to correlate with a longer response, though no exposure-response relationship was observed for the duration of response.
L2028: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L2029: FDA agrees; however, the analyses above are for modeling purposes only and should not be considered as an FDA agreement of any anti-tumor effects of nirogacestat in other tumor type (beyond DT as described above in the clinical section of this review). Additionally, FDA considers all descriptions of statistical significance in the section to be nominal only.
L2030: ## 19.4.11. ER (efficacy) Assessment Summary
L2031: ## The Applicant's Position:
L2032: Exposure-response and PKPDx analyses were conducted using a nirogacestat PopPK model and response data obtained from Studies A8641014 and NIR-DT-301. PKPDx analyses characterized nirogacestat-mediated treatment effects for PFS and tumor size that were independent of pharmacological exposure at the evaluated dose ranges. For analyses of nirogacestat exposure and tumor size, an exposure-response plateau was achieved within the exposure ranges predicted for the \ge 100 mg BID to 150 mg BID dose range. A significant relationship between nirogacestat exposure and OR was not identified at the evaluated dose ranges. There was an apparent trend towards longer responses in the higher exposure group; however, it was not found to be a statistically significant predictor of DOR.

Final Model Parameters	Summary	Acceptability [FDA's comments]
Model Structure	The relationships between nirogacestat exposure and efficacy and endpoints were evaluated as PKPDx or ERR models. The PKPDx model development for PFS and tumor size used individual parameter estimates	Yes.
PK/PD model for tumor size (RECIST or tumor volume)
L2034: from the final PopPK model added to the PKPDx datasets. For the ERR analyses of DOR and BOR, the final PopPK model was used to calculate individual estimates of exposure at nominal steady state.
The treatment effect and exposure-response models were parameterized as follows (shown here including covariate effects):
Efficacy analysis:
Treatment effect: h(t) = h_0(t) * \exp(\text{Effect} * I_{\text{Nirogacestat}} + b_1 * (\text{Cov}-\text{median}) + b_{2i} * I_{\text{Cov}i})
Exposure-response: h(t) = h_0(t) * \exp(\alpha * (\text{Exposure}-\text{Median Exposure}) + b_1 * (\text{Cov}-\text{median}) + b_{2i} * I_{\text{Cov}i})
where h_0(t) is the hazard in the participant with nominal covariate effects. For the treatment effect model, h_0(t) is the hazard for placebo-treated participants. For the exposure-response model, h_0(t) is the hazard for participants with median exposure. Relative risk (h(t)=h_0(t)) is provided by the righthand-side of the equations for specified treatment or exposure and covariate values. Exposure is the estimated nominal steady state AUC for each nirogacestat-treated participant and 0 for placebo-treated participants, and \alpha is the parameter estimate of the slope of the exposure-response relationship. I_{\text{Nirogacestat}} is a binary variable with value 0 for placebo participants and value 1 for nirogacestat treated participants. Effect is the parameter estimate of the treatment effect of nirogacestat over placebo. For continuous covariates, a slope (b_1) was estimated for the median-centered covariate (Cov). For categorical covariates, a separate effect (b_{2i}) was estimated for each categorical value of the covariate (I_{\text{Cov}i}).
was characterized by a first-order tumor growth (evaluated with placebo data) and first order elimination by nirogacestat treatment.

Model Parameter Estimates	PFS, tumor volume, and RECIST tumor size	Yes
Model Evaluation	Final PKPDx models were determined based on maximized likelihood (lowest stable OFV), physiological plausibility of parameter values, successful numerical convergence, parameter precision and acceptable VPC.	Yes.
The PK/PD model for tumor dynamics described data reasonably well.
Covariates and Clinical Relevance	Neither age nor sex had an impact on PFS or tumor size. Tumor type was a significant covariate on the hazard function for PFS. Baseline tumor size was modeled as a parameter in both tumor size models; however, genotypes had no impact in either model, and tumor type had no impact in the RECIST model.	Yes

L2061: ## 19.4.12. ER (safety) Executive Summary
L2062: PK/PDx models were developed to evaluate the PK/PDx and exposure-response relationship (ERR) between nirogacestat and efficacy endpoints in participants with DT, which included the following objectives:
L2063: - Characterize the PopPKPDx relationship of nirogacestat exposure and changes in FSH over time;
- Characterize the PopPKPDx relationship of nirogacestat exposure and OD;
L2064: - Characterize the ERR between nirogacestat exposure and the incidence of the Grade \ge 3 diarrhea, Grade \ge 3 hypophosphatemia, Grade \ge 3 rash, Grade \ge 3 treatment-related AEs, any treatment-related AE, any treatment-related SAE, any treatment-related AE leading to dose modification, Grade \ge 3 TEAEs, any TEAE, any TESAE, and any TEAE leading to dose modification;
- Characterize the ERR between nirogacestat exposure and the duration of Grade \ge 3 hypophosphatemia, Grade \ge 3 diarrhea, Grade \ge 3 rash, and OD.
L2065: **Methods:** The previously developed nirogacestat PopPK model was used to derive individual exposure parameters. Nonlinear mixed effects modeling was employed to develop PKPDx models to characterize safety and efficacy of nirogacestat in participants with DT, participants with solid tumors other than DT, and participants with T-ALL/LBL. The binary outcome OD was analyzed using a time to event model, whereas FSH and phosphate maximum reduction from baseline were analyzed as continuous variables. Model development was carried out sequentially, first describing disease progression and placebo response, followed by assessment of the nirogacestat ERR. Covariate-parameter relationships of interest were explored graphically and formally tested if substantial trends were evident. Final PKPDx models were qualified by VPCs, in addition to numerical checks of parameter precision and model robustness.
L2066: Bayesian posthoc PK parameter estimates were used to estimate nirogacestat exposures (nominal steady state or exposure at day of onset) for the subsequent exposure-response analysis. For each safety endpoint, nominal steady state exposure was used for participants with no event. To assess the impact of changes in exposure for binary safety endpoints, exposure-response relationships were graphically evaluated in all participants and in nirogacestat treated participants only. Significant relationships were further examined by comparing treatment effect models (i.e., to assess response for nirogacestat treated participants compared to placebo participants) to exposure-response models (i.e., to assess changes in response as nirogacestat concentration changes). Safety endpoints were evaluated by logistic regression.
L2067: **Results:** A total of 73 WOCBP were available for FSH evaluation, all of which were participants with DT from Study NIR-DT-301. FSH was found to linearly increase with nirogacestat serum concentrations. The delay between serum concentrations and FSH change was characterized by an indirect response model. A time-dependent drug effect was used to characterize the reduction of FSH observed in some participants. Variability in FSH, drug effect, and time-dependent drug effect decay were relatively high and could not be further explained by covariates.
L2068: A total of 82 WOCBP were available for OD evaluations, including 76 participants with DT, 3 with solid tumors other than DT, 2 with T-ALL/LBL, and 1 participant with a missing
L2069: description for tumor type. Time to OD occurrence was related to nirogacestat treatment, independent of nirogacestat exposures, at the evaluated dose range. The HR for nominal treatment relative to placebo was estimated to be 3.3. No relationships between OD and population or age were observed.
L2070: A total of 214 participants were available for evaluation of AE endpoints. Eleven endpoints were available for safety analysis: Grade \ge 3 diarrhea, Grade \ge 3 hypophosphatemia, Grade \ge 3 rash, Grade \ge 3 treatment-related AEs, any treatment-related AE, any treatment-related SAE, any treatment-related AE leading to dose modification, Grade \ge 3 TEAEs, any TEAE, any TESAE, and any TEAE leading to dose modification. Nirogacestat exposure was found to be a significant predictor of any Grade \ge 3 TEAE, though further exposure response models showed that this was a treatment effect rather than an exposure effect. Of these binary endpoints, only Grade \ge 3 hypophosphatemia was found to have a relationship with nirogacestat exposure. Compared to treatment, C_{max} was found to be the best predictor of Grade \ge 3 hypophosphatemia occurrence. At a 100 mg dose, the probability of a Grade \ge 3 hypophosphatemia occurrence was calculated to be 5.89%. For a 150 mg dose, the probability of a Grade \ge 3 hypophosphatemia occurrence was calculated to be 6.78%.
L2071: Dose modifications, such as dose interruptions (or holds) and dose reductions, during Study NIR-DT-301 DB phase or OLE phase were also compared against nominal nirogacestat exposure at steady state. Both C_{min} and AUC were found to be significant predictors of dose modifications for NIR-DT-301, with higher exposure predictive of a subject decreasing their dose, though C_{max} was not.
L2072: Phosphate maximum reduction from baseline was modeled by linear and nonlinear regression. Treatment effect, linear, exponential, E_{max}, and sigmoid E_{max} models were tested with and without an intercept. Models with an intercept were found to be more significant than those without due to the non-zero phosphate change from baseline seen in the placebo group. For the nirogacestat treated group, at treatment effect model was found to best describe the relationship of phosphate change from baseline and nirogacestat.
L2073: The duration of four endpoints (Grade \ge 3 diarrhea, Grade \ge 3 hypophosphatemia, Grade \ge 3 rash, and OD) were explored through Kaplan-Meier plots. No significant ERR relationships were detected within the range of nirogacestat exposure.
L2074: **Conclusion:** This analysis represents a comprehensive examination of PKPDx data collected from a Phase 1 study of nirogacestat in participants with advanced solid tumors and participants with relapsed or refractory T-ALL/LBL, and a Phase 3 trial of nirogacestat in participants with DT. Principal findings of the safety analyses are described below:
L2075: - FSH linearly increased with nirogacestat serum concentrations in WOCBP. The temporal difference between nirogacestat serum concentrations and FSH changes was described
L2076: by an indirect response model. A reduction of nirogacestat effect on FSH over time was detected. Although the typical half-life attenuation was 49 weeks, the effect was associated with high variability.
L2077: - A nirogacestat treatment effect on OD occurrence was observed, which was exposure independent at the evaluated dose range of 80 mg BID to 330 mg BID. The HR for nominal treatment relative to placebo was estimated to be 3.3.
- A statistically significant and plausible ERR (p &lt; 0.05) was observed for Grade \ge 3 hypophosphatemia. Compared to treatment, C_{max} was found to be the best predictor of Grade \ge 3 hypophosphatemia occurrence. At a 100 mg dose, the probability of a Grade \ge 3 hypophosphatemia occurrence was calculated to be 5.9%. For a 150 mg dose, the probability of a Grade \ge 3 hypophosphatemia occurrence was calculated to be 6.78%. No ERRs were observed for the other binary safety events.
- A Treatment effect model with an intercept was found to best describe the relationship of phosphate maximum reduction from baseline and nirogacestat.
- Sixty participants were found to have dose modifications during Study NIR-DT-301 or OLE. Nominal nirogacestat exposure at steady state (C_{min} and AUC) was found to be a significant predictor (p &lt; 0.05) of dose modifications.
- No ERRs were detected within the range of nirogacestat exposure for the duration of Grade \ge 3 diarrhea, Grade \ge 3 hypophosphatemia, Grade \ge 3 rash, or OD.
L2078: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L2079: FDA agrees. Positive E-R relationships for Grade 3 hypophosphatemia was well characterized over the dose range from 20 mg BID to 330 mg BID. We added the following statement in section 12.2 in the USPI label.
L2080: ## Exposure-Response Relationships
L2081: *There is an exposure response relationship between OGSIVEO exposure and Grade 3 hypophosphatemia with a higher risk of Grade 3 hypophosphatemia at higher exposure. Additionally, increased rate of Grade 3 hypophosphatemia occurred more frequently with higher nirogacestat doses (0.13 to 2.2 times the recommended dose).*
L2082: ## 19.4.13. ER (safety) Assessment Summary
L2083: ## The Applicant's Position:
L2084: Exposure-response and PKPD analyses were conducted using a nirogacestat PopPK model and safety data obtained from Studies A8641014 and NIR-DT-301. A linear relationship between FSH and nirogacestat serum concentration in WOCBP was observed. However, high variability in
L2085: the drug effect, presumably due to menstrual cycle oscillation and sparse sampling, made precise estimation of drug effects difficult. A nirogacestat treatment effect was described for OD occurrence, which was also independent of exposure at the evaluated dose ranges. Significant relationships between nirogacestat exposure and Grade \ge 3 hypophosphatemia were described, with a 0.91% absolute increase in risk estimated at the 150 mg dose compared to the 100 mg dose. Finally, a statistically significant relationship between nirogacestat exposure (trough concentration and AUCtau) and dose modification was identified; however, the relationship with Cmax was not statistically significant. This suggests that the need for dose modifications may be associated with inter-individual differences in CL. Thus, individuals with higher nirogacestat CL are less likely to require dose reductions or interruptions than individuals with lower nirogacestat CL.

L2088: Model Structure	
The relationships between nirogacestat exposure and safety endpoints were evaluated as PKPDx or ERR models. The PKPDx model development for OD and FSH used individual parameter estimates from the final PopPK model added to the PKPDx datasets. For the ERR analyses of safety endpoints evaluated by logistic regression, the final PopPK model was used to calculate individual estimates of exposure on the day of onset, as available. For each safety endpoint, nominal steady state exposure was used for participants with no event. Logistic regression models tested covariates on the model intercept only. Covariates were tested in a stepwise forward search at a p < 0.05 level of significance. Only covariates that were significant in a univariate search were included in multivariate testing. The treatment effect and exposure-response models were parameterized as follows (shown here including covariate effects):
Treatment effect: Pr(\text{Response}) = \text{invlogit}(\text{Intercept} + \text{Effect} * I_{\text{nirogacestat}} + b1 * (\text{Cov}-\text{median}) + b2_i * I_{\text{Cov}i})
Exposure-response: Pr(\text{Response}) = \text{invlogit}(\text{Intercept} + \text{Slope} * \text{Exposure} + b1 * (\text{Cov}-\text{median}) + b2_i * I_{\text{Cov}i})
where I_{\text{nirogacestat}} is a binary variable with value 0 for placebo participants and value 1 for nirogacestat treated participants. Effect is the parameter estimate for the treatment effect of nirogacestat over placebo. Exposure is the estimated nominal steady state AUC for each participant treated with nirogacestat and 0 for placebo participants. Slope is the parameter estimate of the slope of exposure-response relationship. For continuous covariates, a slope (b1) was estimated for the median-centered covariate (Cov). For categorical covariates, a separate effect (b2_i) was estimated for each categorical value of the covariate (I_{\text{Cov}i}). FSH was described with a linear relationship with nirogacestat serum concentration using an indirect response model. The probability of OD was described by a parametric time-to-event model (Gompertz hazard) comprising an effect of nirogacestat treatment on the hazard. Probability of Grade \ge 3 hypophosphatemia and dose modifications were described by logistic regression. Phosphate maximum reduction from baseline was described with a treatment effect model with an intercept. No ERR could be
Yes.
PK/PD models for AEs of interest (FSH and OD) were considered exploratory for reasons detailed in 19.4.3.5 ER review issues.
L2089: identified for any of the other safety endpoints evaluated.

Model Evaluation	Final PKPDx models were determined based on maximized likelihood (lowest stable OFV), physiological plausibility of parameter values, successful numerical convergence, parameter precision and acceptable VPC.	Yes

L2117: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L2118: FDA verified the Applicant's analysis and in general agreed with the Applicant's position. The PK/PD model for tumor size adequately characterized longitudinal patient data that included two intra-patient doses. The observed longitudinal tumor size data supports that efficacy should not be affected by dose reduction. The PK/PD model was considered reliable to provide further support for the dose reduction. PK/PD models for FSH level and ovarian dysfunction are limited to inform dose modification from safety perspective due to sparse/incongruent data. However, positive E-R relationships for Grade 3 hypophosphatemia was well characterized over the dose range from 20 mg BID to 330 mg BID.
L2119: Taken together, the longitudinal tumor PK/PD model, positive ER relationship for dose modification and hypophosphatemia, as well as positive trend in E-R for FSH and Grade 3+ TEAE, suggest benefits in improving safety and tolerability with a lower exposure.
L2120: ## **19.4.14. ER Review Issues**
L2121: ## E-R for ORR with steady-state exposure
L2122: A relatively flat E-R relationship excluding placebo between steady-state exposure and response was observed which could be due to small exposure range for a single dose. Given dose reduction occurred in about half of the patients with DT, the Applicant was asked to perform additional E-R analysis with average exposure till response (for responders) or the end of treatment (for non-responders). Similar to Figure 13, a flat E-R relationship was observed,
L2123: suggesting that response was retained despite a lower average exposure due to dose reduction (Figure 24).
L2124: **Figure 24:** Logistic Regression Relationships for Objective Response for Patients with DT and Patients with DT treated with Nirogacestat
L2125: Source: Response to FDA IR 07/19/2023 Figure 2.
L2126: ## PK/PD for AEs of interest are considered exploratory or inconclusive
L2127: PK/PD model for FSH elevation was described by an indirect response model where the drug effect with a time-dependent decay was parameterized on the generation of FSH. This model is limited due to the sparse sampling and lab collected with no regards to monstrous cycle. As a result, the intra- and inter- individual variabilities of the model are large, and the model does not describe the data well. The exploratory analysis for ovarian dysfunction characterized by FSH level and clinical symptoms is subject to the same issues. The details of these models were thus not provided in this review. Despite the poor model fitness, there was a clear treatment effect on inducing FSH level or ovarian dysfunction. Whether the effect was concentration dependent is inconclusive due to the stated issues.
L2128: ## 19.4.15. Reviewer's Independent Analysis
L2129: ## Efficacy does not appear to be affected by dose reduction
L2130: Dose modifications in patients with DT with 150 mg daily nirogacestat were primarily due to acute Grade 3+ TEAEs such as diarrhea, rash, and hypophosphatemia. Chronic TEAEs including Grade 2+ premature menopause or primary ovarian insufficiency also led to dose modifications. As shown in Figure 25, a significant proportion of patients had dose reduction as early as 1st cycle and this proportion continued to increase over time.
L2131: To assess if efficacy is affected, an exploratory analysis PFS was visualized in patients with dose reduction which was not found to differ from those whose dose was unchanged (Figure 26). In an additional exploratory analysis, tumor shrinkage appeared to correlate with PFS (Figure 27). Tumor size summarized by real-time dose in Study NIR-DT-301 showed comparable slope of tumor size decrease between patients with and without dose reduction.
L2132: These results however do not provide information regarding whether the timing of dose reduction affects tumor shrinkage. To visualize temporal relationship between dose reduction and tumor dynamics, patients were further grouped by dose reduction occurring in the following time frames: &lt;12 weeks, 12-&lt;24 weeks, 24-&lt;48 weeks, &gt;=48 weeks. Considering that tumor size was captured in a time frame for each planned visit and there were nonuniform missing records amongst patients, reviewer used PK/PD model to simulate tumor size every 4 weeks based on patient's individual parameter estimates. The predicted tumor dynamic profiles overlaid with observations for all patients (Figure 28).
L2133: For different dose reduction categories (i.e., &lt;12 weeks, 12-&lt;24 weeks, 24-&lt;48 weeks, &gt;=48 weeks), the median tumor size reductions over time were summarized. As shown in Figure 29, all groups that were dose reduced to 100 mg had steeper slopes of tumor size reduction compared to the group that stayed on 150 mg. This trend was observed with no regards to the time frame in which dose reduction occurred, suggesting that efficacy was retained with dose reduction including those occurred early. This observation aligns with the modeling results that a treatment effect independent of concentrations was identified as a predictor for tumor dynamics.
L2134: Longitudinal PK/PD model for tumor dynamics included two intra-patient doses and thus data were considered robust for evaluating concentration dependent effect on tumor shrinkage in patients with dose reduction. However, one cannot conclude the absence of concentration dependent response in all patients because the analysis of exposure response is determined by the data from patients who underwent dose reduction. These patients could have an increased sensitivity to drug for PK (e.g., high free drug concentrations not reflected by total drug concentrations) and/or PD (e.g., elevated biochemical characteristics) reasons compared to other patients whose dose was unchanged.
L2135: Given the very high percentage of protein binding for nirogacestat (99.4%), variability in this percentage may lead to a large difference in free drug concentrations. The possibility of increased free drug in patients with dose reduction could not be verified since it was not measured. To evaluate a potential PD difference, known patient factors were modeled as covariates and none were found statistically significant. For unknown confounding factors, randomized study evaluating two doses is warranted to interrogate the exposure-response relationship. Therefore, the flat exposure response should only be interpreted in the scope of data used to develop the PK/PD model. Although it does not provide conclusive evidence that a lower starting dose could be as effective as the 150 mg BID, the flat ER relationship observed in
L2136: the PK/PD model for tumor size, along with observed data supports maintenance of efficacy in patients who need dose reduction in response to AEs. The onset of AEs may have reflected the possibly increased sensitivity to the drug whether it is attributed to PK and/or PD differences in those patients.

L2147: ## 19.4.16. Overall Benefit-Risk Evaluation based on E-R Analyses
L2148: ## The Applicant's Position:
L2149: Exposure-response relationships were explored for several efficacy and safety endpoints using data from Studies A8641014 and NIR-DT-301, and the final model from the PopPK Analysis. An
L2150: exposure-response plateau was achieved within the predicted exposure ranges (AUC_{\tau} and C_{min}) for the \ge 100 mg to 150 mg dose range. Reduction in tumor size was independent of nirogacestat exposure within the evaluated dose range, whether it was determined by volumetric MRI (NIR-DT-301 only) or using RECIST (by CT or MRI). Similarly, there was a treatment effect relationship with PFS that was independent of exposure within the evaluated dose ranges. Additional analyses were conducted to explore if a relationship between nirogacestat exposure and objective response could be described; however, exposure was not found to be a significant predictor of duration of response due to low number of participants with loss of response.
L2151: A statistically significant relationship was found between exposure and dose modifications. Participants with higher predicted exposures (AUC_{\tau} and C_{min}) at steady-state were more likely to have had dose modifications than those with lower steady-state exposures. Given that the majority of dose modifications were due to TEAEs, this relationship may suggest that participants with lower nirogacestat clearance were more likely to experience TEAEs that required a dose modification.
L2152: The exposure-response data support a recommended starting dose of 150 mg for all patients with allowance for dose modification based on tolerability to ensure optimal exposure for each individual.
L2153: ## The FDA's Assessment:
L2154: FDA agrees.
L2155: ## 19.4.17. Physiologically Based Pharmacokinetic Modeling Analysis
L2156: ## Executive Summary
L2157: The objective of this review is to evaluate the adequacy of the Applicant's following PBPK report to support the intended uses.
L2158: *"Development of a PBPK model for nirogacestat with the Simcyp population-based simulator and subsequent evaluation of DDI liability as a victim and perpetrator of CYP3A4."*
L2159: The Division of Pharmacometrics has reviewed the PBPK report and supporting modeling files and concluded the followings.
L2160: - The nirogacestat model is adequate to predict the nirogacestat PK profiles following a single intravenous dose (0.0435 mg), a single oral dose administration (1, 2.5, 5, 10, 25, 40, 50, 65 120 and 150 mg), or multiple oral dose administration (7 and 40 mg BID) in healthy subjects
L2161: and multiple oral dose administration (20, 40, 80, 100, 130, 150 and 220 mg BID) in cancer patients.
L2162: - The nirogacestat model is adequate to predict the effect of itraconazole and ketoconazole (strong CYP3A4 inhibitors) on the PK of nirogacestat following multiple dose administration of nirogacestat (100 mg or 150 mg BID). Model predicted nirogacestat geometric mean AUC ratio was around 5-7-fold in the presence of itraconazole or ketoconazole in healthy subjects.
- The nirogacestat model is adequate to predict the effect of fluconazole, clarithromycin and erythromycin (moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors) on the PK of nirogacestat following multiple dose administration of nirogacestat (100 mg or 150 mg BID). Model predicted nirogacestat geometric mean AUC ratio was around 3-fold in the presence of fluconazole, clarithromycin or erythromycin in healthy subjects.
- The nirogacestat model is adequate to predict the effect of cimetidine (a weak CYP3A4 inhibitor) on the PK of nirogacestat following multiple dose administration of nirogacestat (100 mg or 150 mg BID). Model predicted nirogacestat geometric mean AUC ratio was about 1.4-fold in the presence of cimetidine in healthy subjects.
- The nirogacestat model is adequate to predict the effect of rifampicin (a strong CYP3A4 inducer) and efavirenz (a moderate CYP3A4 inducer) on the PK of nirogacestat following multiple dose administration of nirogacestat (100 mg or 150 mg BID). The model predicted nirogacestat exposure decreased about 85% and 67% when coadministered with rifampicin and efavirenz, respectively, in healthy subjects.
- The nirogacestat model is adequate to predict the effect of nirogacestat on the PK of midazolam (a sensitive CYP3A4 substrate) following multiple dose administration of nirogacestat (100 mg or 150 mg BID). The model predicted midazolam exposure increased about 100% when coadministered with nirogacestat in healthy subjects.
- The nirogacestat PBPK model is adequate to predict the effect of nirogacestat on the PK of rosiglitazone (a CYP2C8 substrate) and warfarin (a CYP2C9 substrate) following multiple dose administration of nirogacestat (100 mg and 150 mg, BID). Model predicted changes in the exposure of rosiglitazone and warfarin were not considered clinically significant when coadministered with multiple doses of nirogacestat in healthy subjects.
- In vitro study and PBPK modeling efforts suggest that the DDI potential of nirogacestat (100 mg or 150 mg BID) with mephenytoin and omeprazole (CYP2C19 substrates) cannot be excluded.

L2164: ## Clinical PK and DDI Data

L2167: ## PBPK software
L2168: Simcyp V19 (Simcyp Ltd, UK) was used by the Applicant to develop the PBPK models and assess the DDI liability. The reviewer used the Simcyp V21 for analyses. The simulation results for the PK profiles of nirogacestat and DDI assessment using Simcyp V21 were not significant difference when compared to those generated using Simcyp V19. As a result, the Applicant's simulation results were included in the review.
L2169: ## Model development
L2170: PBPK model parameters are listed in Appendix.
L2171: The nirogacestat model consisted of a first order absorption model and a whole-body PBPK model. The absorption rate constant (ka) was estimated to be 2.899 1/h based on the permeability data in MDCK cells. The lag time (Tlag) of 0.40 hr was assumed based on gastric emptying time under fasted state.
L2172: The f_{u,gut} (unbound fraction in enterocytes) was set to be 1.0. The P_{eff,man} (permeability in man) was predicted to be 6.64 \times 10^{-4} cm/s based on the permeability data in MDCK cells.
L2173: The whole-body full PBPK model was used to simulate nirogacestat distribution. The volume of distribution (V_{ss}, 3.55 L/kg) was predicted using Method 2 in Simcyp and subsequently optimized based on the observed nirogacestat PK in healthy subjects using a global K_p scalar of 0.29. The plasma protein binding (f_u) is about 0.4%.
L2174: Nirogacestat clearance was estimated to be 13.4 L/h after intravenous administration of nirogacestat in Study NIR-DT-102. CYP3A4 is the primary enzyme responsible for the metabolism of nirogacestat. The f_{mCYP3A4} value of 0.857 was estimated based on HLM chemical inhibition data. The rest of the metabolism was assigned as the additional human liver
L2175: microsome clearance (14.3%). Renal elimination was not considered in the model because the majority of nirogacestat clearance was attributed to the metabolic elimination, with minimal drug excreted unchanged in urine.
L2176: The induction parameter (Indmax and IndC50) values of CYP3A4, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9 and CYP2C19 (b) (4). The K_I (8.46 \mu M) value for CYP3A4 time-dependent inhibition was optimized based on data in clinical study following multiple dosing of 95 mg QD nirogacestat (study A8641002), and K_{inact} (4.57 1/h) value for CYP3A4 time-dependent inhibition was obtained from in vitro study (Study 103822). The CYP3A4 competition inhibition parameter value (K_i=0.04 mM) applied in the model was refined based on the DDI study results with midazolam (Study A8641002) by the reviewer.
L2177: ## **Victim and perpetrator drug models**
L2178: The default PBPK models of itraconazole, ketoconazole, fluconazole, clarithromycin, erythromycin, cimetidine, rifampicin efavirenz, midazolam, bupropion, rosiglitazone, warfarin, mephenytoin and omeprazole in SimCYP V19 were used without any modification for DDI prediction. Default healthy volunteer population and cancer population in SimCYP V19 were used in simulations.
L2179: ## **PBPK model application**
L2180: The developed PBPK model was used to simulate the DDI for nirogacestat in the following scenarios.
L2181: - To predict the effects of itraconazole and ketoconazole (strong CYP3A inhibitors) and fluconazole and clarithromycin (moderate CYP3A inhibitors) and cimetidine (a weak CYP3A4 inhibition) on the PK of nirogacestat.
- To predict the effect of rifampicin (a strong CYP3A inducer) and efavirenz (a moderate CYP3A inducer) on the PK of nirogacestat.
- To predict the effect of nirogacestat on the PK of midazolam (a sensitive CYP3A4 substrate).
- To predict the effect of nirogacestat on the PK of bupropion (a CYP2B6 substrate).
- To predict the effect of nirogacestat on the PK of rosiglitazone (a CYP2C8 substrate) and warfarin (a substrate of CYP2C9).
- To predict the effect of nirogacestat on the PK of mephenytoin and omeprazole (CYP2C19 substrates).
L2182: ## Results
L2183: ## 1. Can nirogacestat PBPK model describe nirogacestat PK in healthy subjects?
L2184: **Yes.** Table 79 showed the Applicant's model verification results. The PK profiles after multiple oral doses of 95 mg QD nirogacestat in healthy subjects was used to verify the net auto DDI
L2185: effect (TDI and induction on CYP3A4) on the PK of nirogacestat. The in vitro obtained parameter values for mechanism-based inhibition and induction on CYP3A4 were included in the model. The in vitro KI value (reduced by 5-fold) was optimized to capture the nirogacestat PK profile on day 1 and day 14 after multiple oral doses of 95 mg QD nirogacestat in healthy subjects. In general, the model was able to capture the nirogacestat PK profiles following a single or multiple dose administration (Figure 30). The simulated geometric mean AUC and Cmax values for nirogacestat were within 0.7-1.3-fold of the observed values after a single or multiple dose administration in healthy subjects or cancer patients, except for the dose groups where the number of subjects per dose level is less than 8 (Table 79). It was noted that the PK parameters for nirogacestat tended to have substantial inter-subject variability since most geometric CV values are greater than 30% and even approach 100% in some studies. Therefore, considering the limited number of subjects in some dose groups and the significant variability, the Applicant's model is deemed to be acceptable for the purpose of DDI potential evaluation as a victim. The uncertainty associated with the DDI potential evaluation as a perpetrator will be further assessed using sensitivity analyses.

L2190: Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as "Data" and "The Applicant's Position" are completed by the Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
L2191: ## **2. Can nirogacestat PBPK model predict its effect on midazolam (a sensitive CYP3A4 substrate) PK following multiple dose administration of nirogacestat (100 mg and 150 mg BID)?**
L2192: **Yes.** The nirogacestat mediated combined induction and inhibition effects on CYP3A4 (net inhibition effect) was verified based on the clinical DDI study with midazolam following multiple dose administration of nirogacestat (95 mg QD) (Table 80). The verified model was then used to predict the effect of nirogacestat (100 mg BID and 150 mg BID) on the PK of midazolam. The model predicted about 84% and 107% increase in the exposure of midazolam after coadministration of 100 mg BID and 150 mg BID nirogacestat, respectively, in healthy subjects (Table 80).
L2193: ## **3. Can nirogacestat PBPK model predict the effect of itraconazole and ketoconazole (strong CYP3A inhibitors) on the PK of nirogacestat following multiple dose administration of nirogacestat (100 mg and 150 mg BID) in healthy subjects?**
L2194: **Yes.** The contribution of CYP3A4 to the overall clearance of nirogacestat was estimated based on invitro data and verified based on the clinical DDI study data of single dose nirogacestat with itraconazole (Table 80). The verified model was then used to estimate the effect of strong CYP3A4 inhibitors on the PK of multiple dose nirogacestat. The model predicted nirogacestat (100 mg BID and 150 mg BID) exposure increased 7.02, and 6.33-fold and 5.56 and 5.19-fold when coadministered with itraconazole and ketoconazole, respectively, in healthy subjects (Table 80).
L2195: ## **4. Can nirogacestat PBPK model predict the effect of fluconazole, clarithromycin and erythromycin (moderate CYP3A inhibitors) and cimetidine (a weak CYP3A4 inhibitor) on the PK of nirogacestat following multiple dose administration of nirogacestat (100 mg and 150 mg BID) in healthy subjects?**
L2196: **Yes.** The contribution of CYP3A4 to the overall clearance of nirogacestat was estimated based on invitro data and verified based on the clinical DDI study data of single dose nirogacestat with itraconazole (Table 80). The verified model was then used to estimate the effect of moderate or weak CYP3A4 inhibitors on the PK of nirogacestat. The model predicted nirogacestat (100 mg BID and 150 mg BID) exposure increased about 3.26 and 3.18-fold, 3.83 and 3.46-fold, 3.00 and 2.73-fold, and 1.40 and 1.40-fold when coadministered with fluconazole, clarithromycin, erythromycin and cimetidine, respectively, in healthy subject.
L2197: ## **5. Can nirogacestat PBPK model predict the effect of rifampicin (a strong CYP3A4 inducer) and efavirenz (a moderate CYP3A4 inducer) on the PK of nirogacestat following multiple dose administration of nirogacestat (100 mg and 150 mg BID) in healthy subjects?**
L2198: **Yes.** The contribution of CYP3A4 to the overall clearance of nirogacestat was estimated based on invitro data and verified based on the clinical DDI study data of single dose nirogacestat with itraconazole (Table 80). The verified model was then used to estimate the effect of CYP3A4
L2199: inducers on the PK of nirogacestat. The model predicted nirogacestat exposure decreased about 85% and 67% when coadministered with rifampicin and efavirenz, respectively, in healthy subjects (Table 80).
L2200: 6. Can nirogacestat PBPK model predict the effect of nirogacestat on the PK of bupropion (a CYP2B6 substrate) following multiple dose administration of nirogacestat (100 mg BID and 150 mg BID) in healthy subjects?
L2201: No. The bupropion substrate models have not been adequately validated; therefore, it cannot be used to evaluate the potential effect of nirogacestat on the PK of CYP2B6 substrate.
L2202: 7. Can nirogacestat PBPK model predict the effect of nirogacestat on the PK of rosiglitazone (a CYP2C8 substrate) and warfarin (a substrate of CYP2C9) following multiple dose administration of nirogacestat (100 mg BID and 150 mg BID) in healthy subjects?
L2203: Yes. In vitro studies indicated that nirogacestat is a CYP2C8 and CYP2C9 inducer with an in vitro determined Indmax and IndC50 values of 11.14 and 2.38 \muM for CYP2C8 induction and 12.33 and 4.96 \muM for CYP2C9 induction, respectively. The model predicted rosiglitazone and warfarin exposures were not significantly affected by concomitant use of nirogacestat based on in vitro determined enzyme induction parameter values (AUCR \sim 1.00 for both rosiglitazone and warfarin. The Applicant further conducted a sensitivity analysis of nirogacestat Indmax values to assess the effect on the predicted rosiglitazone and warfarin exposure. Due to the uncertainties regarding the in vitro to in vivo extrapolation of the induction parameter, reviewer also conducted additional sensitivity analysis of nirogacestat IndC50 values. The simulated rosiglitazone and warfarin AUC ratios were around 1.0 with 10-fold lower Ind50 values or 10-fold higher Indmax values than the in vitro determined when coadministered with 100 mg BID or 150 mg BID nirogacestat. These results indicated that the induction potential of nirogacestat is low towards CYP2C8 and CYP2C9.
L2204: 8. Can nirogacestat PBPK model predict the effect of nirogacestat on the PK of mephenytoin and omeprazole (CYP2C19 substrates) following multiple dose administration of nirogacestat (100 mg BID and 150 mg BID) in healthy subjects?
L2205: The Applicant used [REDACTED] (b) (4) Indmax ([REDACTED] (b) (4) and IndC50 ([REDACTED] (b) (4) \muM) values to assess the effect of nirogacestat on the PK of mephenytoin and omeprazole. In addition, the effect of nirogacestat on the PK of mephenytoin and omeprazole was evaluated using a sensitivity analysis of [REDACTED] (b) (4) fold higher nirogacestat Indmax values. These modelling efforts showed a positive induction effect of nirogacestat [REDACTED] (b) (4) on the PK of mephenytoin and omeprazole (Table 80). Due to the uncertainties regarding [REDACTED] (b) (4) of the induction parameter values, the nirogacestat model was not considered adequate to accurately predict the effect of nirogacestat on the PK of mephenytoin and omeprazole. However, the DDI potential of nirogacestat as a perpetrator with CYP2C19 substrates cannot be excluded.

L2229: Figure 5 displays six plots (A-F) showing the Least Squares (LS) mean (%) change from baseline for various Patient-Reported Outcomes (PROs) in the ITT population, comparing Nirogacestat (blue line) and Placebo (black line) across analysis visits (Baseline, C2, C3, C4, C5, C6, C7, C8, C9, C10, C11, C12, C13, C14, C15, C16, C17, C18, C19, C20, C21, C22, C23, C24, C25). Error bars represent 95% Confidence Intervals (CI). Vertical lines indicate the threshold for Improvement (positive change) and Worsening (negative change).
The plots are:
A: BPI-SF Average Worst Pain Intensity. Nirogacestat shows a mean change of -1.5 (SE 0.3) at Cycle 10, compared to Placebo's -0.4 (SE 0.3). The Difference is -1.5 (P<0.001).
B: GODDESS DTSS: Total Symptom Score. Nirogacestat shows a mean change of -1.2 (SE 0.2) at Cycle 10, compared to Placebo's -0.4 (SE 0.2). The Difference is -0.8 (P<0.001).
C: GODDESS DTIS: Physical Functioning Domain Score. Nirogacestat shows a mean change of -0.7 (SE 0.1) at Cycle 10, compared to Placebo's 0.2 (SE 0.1). The Difference is -0.9 (P<0.001).
D: EORTC QLQ-C30: Physical Functioning. Nirogacestat shows a mean change of 8.3 (SE 1.8) at Cycle 10, compared to Placebo's -4.8 (SE 2.0). The Difference is 13.5 (P<0.001).
E: EORTC QLQ-C30: Role Functioning. Nirogacestat shows a mean change of 11.3 (SE 3.1) at Cycle 10, compared to Placebo's -5.4 (SE 3.4). The Difference is 16.8 (P<0.001).
F: EORTC QLQ-C30: Global Health Status/Quality of Life. Nirogacestat shows a mean change of 4.5 (SE 2.8) at Cycle 10, compared to Placebo's -5.9 (SE 3.2). The Difference is 10.4 (P<0.015).
Below each plot, the number of patients (N) remaining in the Nirogacestat and Placebo groups at each analysis visit is listed.
L2230: Figure 6 is a stacked bar chart illustrating the Available Data Rate and Missing Reasons for EORTC QLQ-C30 across various visits (BL, C02, C03, C04, C05, C06, C07, C08, C09, C10) for two arms: Nirogacestat and Placebo. The Y-axis represents Percent (0 to 100). The X-axis represents Visit. The legend indicates the reasons for missing data: Complete (Blue), Disc: AE (Red), Disc: Other (Green), Disc: Progression (Brown), and Not Done: Unknown (Purple).
The chart shows that the available data rate is high at Baseline (BL) for both arms (around 95-100%). As the study progresses, the available data rate decreases significantly, particularly for the Nirogacestat arm, dropping below 50% by Cycle 10 (C10). The Placebo arm maintains a higher available data rate, generally above 70% across all cycles shown.
The missing data reasons are primarily categorized as Disc: AE (Discontinued due to Adverse Event) and Disc: Progression (Discontinued due to Progression) in the later cycles, especially for the Nirogacestat arm.

L2245: Figure 21 displays six logistic regression plots showing the relationship between exposure metrics (Cmax, Cmin, and AUC) and the probability of Grade 3+ Hypophosphatemia events. The plots are arranged in three rows, corresponding to Cmax, Cmin, and AUC, and two columns, corresponding to "All patients" and "Nirogacestat-treated patients only".
The Y-axis represents the probability of Grade 3+ Hypophosphatemia (ranging from 0.00 to 1.00). The X-axis represents the exposure metric (Cmax in ng/mL, Cmin in ng/mL, or AUC in ng\cdoth/mL).
The legend indicates: Nirogacestat (open circles) and Placebo (open triangles).
In all six plots, the data points are stratified by nirogacestat exposure quartile. The solid black points represent the mean exposure and event rates in the placebo treated patients. Vertical black bars represent the 5th to 95th percentile CI on the event rate. The solid blue line indicates the logistic regression model fit. The shaded gray region represents the 95% CI on the modeled event rate. The p-value for the slope is indicated as p < 0.05 in all plots.
The top row (Cmax) shows 19 events/208 subjects for All patients and 19 events/164 subjects for Nirogacestat-treated patients only. The middle row (Cmin) shows 19 events/208 subjects for All patients and 19 events/164 subjects for Nirogacestat-treated patients only. The bottom row (AUC) shows 19 events/208 subjects for All patients and 19 events/164 subjects for Nirogacestat-treated patients only.
L2246: Figure 22 displays three logistic regression plots showing the relationship between nirogacestat exposure metrics (Cmax, Cmin, AUC) and the probability of dose reduction or dose hold (Y-axis, ranging from 0.00 to 1.00) for 94 treated patients (59 events). The data points are represented by green circles, and the solid blue line represents the logistic regression model fit, with the shaded gray region indicating the 95% confidence interval (CI) on the modeled event rate.
The top plot shows the relationship with Cmax (ng/mL). The p-value for the slope is 0.0888.
The middle plot shows the relationship with Cmin (ng/mL). The p-value for the slope is p < 0.05.
The bottom plot shows the relationship with AUC (ng\cdoth/mL). The p-value for the slope is p < 0.05.
Black points and vertical bars represent the mean exposure and event rates stratified by nirogacestat exposure quartile, along with the 5th to 95th percentile CI on the event rate.
L2247: Boxplot showing Phosphate Maximum Reduction From Baseline (%) versus AUC Quartile (0 mg, Q1, Q2, Q3, Q4) for Nirogacestat and Placebo treated patients. The Y-axis ranges from 0% to -50%. The X-axis shows AUC Quartiles: 0 mg, Q1 (0, 15.8), Q2 (15.8, 18.8), Q3 (18.8, 27.7), Q4 (27.7, 120). Data points are colored based on Hypophosphatasia3 status: No (green), Yes (blue), NA (gray). The boxes represent the interquartile range and median, and the whiskers extend to 1.5 times the interquartile range. The plot generally shows increasing phosphate reduction with increasing AUC quartile.
L2248: Figure 24 displays two logistic regression plots showing Objective Response (Y-axis, 0.00 to 1.00) versus Cavg (ng/mL) (X-axis, 0 to 750) for patients with DT. The legend indicates dose groups: 0 mg (pink), 80 mg (orange), 100 mg (brown), 110 mg (teal), 150 mg (green), 165 mg (blue), and 220 mg (black). Placebo is marked with a triangle (△).
The left plot, titled "All patients," shows 40 events/147 subjects with a p-value < 0.05. The right plot, titled "Nirogacestat-treated patients only," shows 35 events/103 subjects with a p-value = 0.443. Both plots show a positive trend line and shaded confidence intervals.
L2249: Figure 25 displays two stacked plots showing dose reduction over time in patients with Ductal Tubular (DT) treated with Nirogacestat in Study NIR-DT-301. The X-axis for both plots is "Time since start of treatment (day)".
The left plot shows the "Number of DT patients" (Y-axis, 0 to 75). The right plot shows the "Proportion of DT patients" (Y-axis, 0.00 to 1.00). Both plots include a color scale for DOSE (0 to 150).
Both plots feature vertical dashed red lines indicating the median onset and duration of response. The text annotation reads: "ORR median duration: 14 months".
L2250: Figure 26 is a Kaplan-Meier plot showing Progression-Free Survival (PFS) probability (%) versus Time (months) for Study NIR-DT-301, stratified by Nirogacestat dose reduction status. The X-axis represents Time (0 to 30 months), and the Y-axis represents PFS probability (%).
The plot compares three groups:
Nirogacestat dose reduced (Blue line)
Nirogacestat dose unchanged (Yellow line)
Placebo (Gray line)
The Nirogacestat dose reduced group shows the highest PFS probability, followed by the Nirogacestat dose unchanged group, and the Placebo group shows the lowest PFS probability.
A table below the plot shows the Number at risk for each group at various time points (0, 3, 6, 9, 12, 15, 18, 21, 24, 27, 30 months):
0	3	6	9	12	15	18	21	24	27	30
Nirogacestat dose reduced	33	26	23	20	19	19	17	11	8	5	1
Nirogacestat dose unchanged	36	31	29	29	27	25	24	15	9	7	3
Placebo	72	60	47	40	32	29	25	9	6	4	1
L2251: Figure 27 is a logistic regression plot showing the relationship between Tumor size change from baseline (%) (X-axis) and PFS probability (Y-axis) in Study NIR-DT-301. The X-axis ranges from -100% to 50%, and the Y-axis ranges from 0.00 to 1.00.
The plot displays a fitted logistic curve (blue line) with a shaded confidence interval (gray area). The curve shows that PFS probability increases sharply as tumor size change approaches 0% and becomes positive. Data points are marked with black dots and error bars, representing tumor size change at the PFS event or censoring.
L2252: Figure 28 displays three scatter plots comparing observed tumor size change (CFB) versus time since first dose (wks) for individual patients, overlaid with individual predictions. The plots are for Placebo, Nirogacestat-dose unchanged, and Nirogacestat-dose reduced groups. The Y-axis is CFB (tumor size change from baseline) in percent, ranging from -100% to 50%. The X-axis is Time since First Dose (wks), ranging from 0 to 156. Each color dot (observation) and line (individual prediction) represents an individual patient. The Placebo plot shows a general trend of tumor growth (positive CFB). The Nirogacestat-dose unchanged and Nirogacestat-dose reduced plots show a general trend of tumor shrinkage (negative CFB), with the dose reduced group showing a slightly steeper decline.
L2253: Figure 29 is a scatter plot showing Tumor size change from baseline (%) versus Time since start of treatment (week) for Study NIR-DT-301. The Y-axis ranges from -100% to 100%. The X-axis ranges from 0 to 100 weeks. The plot compares Placebo (No dose reduction) and Nirogacestat across different dose reduction time frames: No dose reduction (N=72), Doser < 12 weeks (N=33), Doser 12<24 weeks (N=5, N=17), Doser 24<48 weeks (N=8), and Doser >=48 weeks (N=6). The Placebo group shows a general trend of tumor growth (positive CFB). The Nirogacestat groups show a general trend of tumor shrinkage (negative CFB), with the dose reduction groups showing varying degrees of shrinkage.
L2254: BEST AVAILABLE COPY
Figure 30 displays observed (dots) and simulated (lines) nirogacestat plasma concentration-time profiles across six panels (A-F).
A (Single IV Dose): Shows concentration over time (0 to 240 h) for 0.0435 mg IV. Observed and simulated concentrations are nearly identical, showing a rapid decline.
B (Single Oral Dose): Shows concentration over time (0 to 480 h) for 150 mg oral dose. Observed and simulated concentrations are nearly identical, showing a rapid decline.
D (Single Ascending Oral Doses): Shows concentration over time (0 to 480 h) for doses 1 mg, 2.5 mg, 5 mg, 10 mg, 25 mg, 40 mg, 50 mg, 65 mg, and 120 mg. Observed and simulated concentrations are nearly identical, showing a rapid decline.
C (Multiple Oral Doses in Healthy Subjects): Shows concentration over time (0 to 480 h) for 95 mg QD (top row) and 40 mg QD (bottom row). Observed and simulated concentrations are nearly identical, showing a rapid decline.
E (Multiple Oral Doses in Healthy Subjects): Shows concentration over time (0 to 480 h) for 7 mg BID (top row) and 40 mg QD (bottom row). Observed and simulated concentrations are nearly identical, showing a rapid decline.
F (Multiple Oral Doses in Patients with Cancer): Shows concentration over time (0 to 480 h) for doses 25 mg BID, 40 mg BID, 80 mg BID, 100 mg BID, 130 mg BID, 150 mg BID, and 220 mg BID. Observed and simulated concentrations are nearly identical, showing a rapid decline.
